Polynomial Furstenberg joinings and its applications Thanks: This research is supported by National Natural Science Foundation of China (12031019, 12090012, 11971455, 11731003).
Wen Huang, Song Shao, Xiangdong Ye

Abstract
. In this paper, a polynomial version of Furstenberg joining is introduced and its structure is investigated. Particularly, it is shown that if all polynomials are non-linear, then almost every ergodic component of the joining is a direct product of an infinity-step pro-nilsystem and a Bernoulli system. As applications, some new convergence theorems are obtained. Particularly, it is proved that if T T and S S are ergodic measure preserving transformations on a probability space ( X , 𝒳 , μ ) (X,\mathcal{X},\mu) and T T has zero entropy, then for all c i ∈ ℤ ∖ { 0 } c_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\} , all integral polynomials p j p_{j} with deg ⁡ p j ≥ 2 \deg{p_{j}}\geq 2 , and for all f i , g j ∈ L ∞ ​ ( X , μ ) f_{i},g_{j}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu) , 1 ≤ i ≤ m 1\leq i\leq m and 1 ≤ j ≤ d 1\leq j\leq d , lim N → ∞ 1 N ∑ n = 0 N − 1 f 1 ( T c 1 ​ n x ) ⋯ f m ( T c m ​ n x ) ⋅ g 1 ( S p 1 ​ ( n ) x ) ⋯ g d ( S p d ​ ( n ) x ) , \lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{1}(T^{c_{1}n}x)\cdots f_{m}(T^{c_{m}n}x)\cdot g_{1}(S^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots g_{d}(S^{p_{d}(n)}x), exists in L 2 ​ ( X , μ ) L^{2}(X,\mu) , which extends the recent result by Host and Frantzikinakis. Moreover, it is shown that for an ergodic measure-preserving system ( X , 𝒳 , μ , T ) (X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T) , a non-linear integral polynomial p p and f ∈ L ∞ ​ ( X , μ ) f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu) , the Furstenberg systems of ( f ⁡ ( T p ⁡ ( n ) ) ​ x ) n ∈ ℤ \big(f(T^{p(n)})x\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}} are ergodic and isomorphic to direct products of infinite-step pro-nilsystems and Bernoulli systems for almost every x ∈ X x\in X , which answers a problem by Frantzikinakis.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 37A05; 37A30

1.
Introduction

In the article, the set of integers (resp. natural numbers
\{1,2,\ldots\}
) is denoted by
\mathbb{Z}
(resp.
\mathbb{N}
).
An
integral polynomial
is a polynomial taking integer values at the
integers. Two polynomials
p(n)
and
q(n)
are
essentially distinct
if
p(n)-q(n)
is not a constant function.
If
n<m\in\mathbb{Z}
, then
[n,m]
denotes the set
\{n,n+1,\ldots,m\}
.

Throughout this paper, all probability spaces
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
considered are Lebesgue, meaning
X
can be given the structure of
a compact metric space and
\mathcal{X}
is its Borel
\sigma
-algebra. By a
measure-preserving system
(m.p.s. for short), we mean a quadruple

(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
, where
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
is a Lebesgue probability space and
T:X\rightarrow X
is an invertible measure preserving transformation.

1.1.
Furstenberg Joining

The notion of joinings was introduced by Furstenberg in his seminal paper
[
15
]
, and now the theory of joinings is an
important part of ergodic theory
[
19
]
. Some joinings play a key role in the study of multiple recurrence and
multiple ergodic averages. To give a dynamical proof of the well-known theorem of Szemerédi
[
39
]
, Furstenberg proved the following multiple recurrence theorem
[
16
]
, which says that for a m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
A\in\mathcal{X}
with positive measure, there is some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\mu(A\cap T^{-n}A\cap T^{-2n}A\cap\cdots\cap T^{-dn}A)>0.

In the same paper, Furstenberg introduced the following measure (now called
Furstenberg joining
):

(1.1)

\mu^{(d)}(\prod_{i=1}^{d}A_{i})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mu(T^{-n}A_{1}\cap T^{-2n}A_{2}\cap\cdots\cap T^{-dn}A_{d}),

for all
A_{1},\ldots,A_{d}\in\mathcal{X}
. Note that the existence of the limit was proved in
[
20
,
43
]
,
and in
[
16
]

\mu^{(d)}
was in fact defined as a limit of a subsequence.
It is easy to verify that
\mu^{(d)}
is invariant under the actions of
T^{(d)}=T\times\cdots\times T
(
d
times) and
\tau_{d}=T\times T^{2}\times\cdots\times T^{d}
. Thus we have a m.p.s.
(X^{d},\mathcal{X}^{d},\mu^{(d)},\tau_{d})
.

In
[
16
]
, it was shown that

\mu^{(d)}
is a conditional product measure in
X^{d}
relative to
(Y_{d-1},\mu_{d-1})
, where
\{(Y_{n},\mu_{n})\}_{n}
is the distal sequence of
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
(
[
17
]
). That is,

(1.2)

\mu^{(d)}=\int_{Y_{d-1}^{d}}\mu_{y_{1}}\times\mu_{y_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mu_{y_{d}}d\mu_{d-1}^{(d)}(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{d}),

where
\mu=\int\mu_{y}\ d\mu_{d-1}(y)
is the disintegration of
\mu
with respect to
\mu_{d-1}
, and

\mu_{d-1}^{(d)}
is defined as (
1.1
) related to
Y_{d-1}
.
This leads to the notion of “characteristic factors”, which was first explicitly defined by Furstenberg and Weiss in
[
18
]
. Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,T)
be a factor of
X
. For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, we say that
Y
is a
characteristic factor of
X
for
\tau_{d}
if for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\prod_{i=1}^{d}f_{i}(T^{in}x)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}(f_{i}|\mathcal{Y})(T^{in}x)\to 0,\ N\to\infty\end{split}

in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
. By (
1.2
) it is easy to see
Y_{d-1}
is a characteristic factor of
X
for
\tau_{d}
.
This enables one to give a reduction of the problem on the multiple recurrence to its distal factors.

In
[
20
]
and in
[
43
]
the authors improved the above result significantly by showing that for an ergodic m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
, its
(d-1)
-step pro-nilfactor is a characteristic factor for
\tau_{d}
, and using this result the authors proved that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{1}(T^{n}x)f_{2}(T^{2n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{dn}x)

exists in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.

1.2.
Polynomial version of the Furstenberg joining

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and let
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{d}\}
be a family of non-constant integral polynomials.
There is a natural way to generalize the Furstenberg joining to the polynomial setting as follows:

(1.3)

\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(d)}(\prod_{i=1}^{d}A_{i})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mu(T^{-p_{1}(n)}A_{1}\cap T^{-p_{2}(n)}A_{2}\cap\cdots\cap T^{-p_{d}(n)}A_{d}),

for all
A_{1},\ldots,A_{d}\in\mathcal{X}
, where the limit exists
[
21
,
31
]
. Of course,
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(d)}
is a probability measure on
X^{d}
and it is
T^{(d)}
-invariant (in fact, it can be proved to be a self-joining). Unlike Furstenberg joining, we can not find some transformation
\tau
related to
\mathcal{A}
like
\tau_{d}
such that
(X^{d},\mathcal{X}^{d},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(d)},\tau)
becomes a m.p.s. Thus to study the dynamical behavior related to polynomials, we need to find another way to generalize the Furstenberg joining.

To overcome the difficulty, when studying the dynamical behavior concerning polynomials
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
, we will consider infinitely
many polynomials
p_{i}^{[k]},1\leq i\leq d,k\in\mathbb{Z}
defined by
p_{i}^{[k]}(n)=p_{i}(n+k)-p_{i}(k),n\in\mathbb{Z}
at the same time. For example, for
\mathcal{A}=\{n^{2},n^{3}\}
, we define a measure
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
on
(X^{2})^{\mathbb{Z}}
as follows: for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
A_{i}^{(1)},A_{i}^{(2)}\in\mathcal{X}
and
-l\leq i\leq l
, put

\begin{split}&\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}\Big((\prod_{j=-\infty}^{-l-1}X^{2})\times\prod_{i=-l}^{l}(A^{(1)}_{i}\times A^{(2)}_{i})\times(\prod_{j=l+1}^{\infty}X^{2})\Big)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mu\Big(\bigcap_{i=-l}^{l}(T^{-(n+i)^{2}}A^{(1)}_{i}\cap T^{-(n+i)^{3}}A^{(2)}_{i})\Big),\end{split}

where the existence of the limit is guaranteed by
[
21
,
31
]
.

Similarly, for
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
, we define a measure
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
on
(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
. This measure

\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is invariant under the product map
T^{\infty}=\cdots\times T^{(d)}\times T^{(d)}\times\cdots
and the shift map
\sigma:(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus for a given m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
and
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
, we associate two m.p.s.

\big((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\sigma\big)\ \text{and}\ \big((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\langle T^{\infty},\sigma\rangle\big),

where
\langle T^{\infty},\sigma\rangle
is the group generated by
\sigma
and
T^{\infty}
. The first one is a
\mathbb{Z}
-system and the second one
is a
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-system, as
\sigma\circ T^{\infty}=T^{\infty}\circ\sigma
.
The measure
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a joining on
(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and we call it an
\infty
-joining with respect to
\mathcal{A}
, see Section
3
for detailed discussions. In this paper, we will focus on the dynamical properties of this joining and give its applications.

Note that in a companion paper by the same authors
[
26
]
, for a given topological system
(X,T)
and
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
, we associate two topological dynamical systems
(W_{x}^{\mathcal{A}},\sigma)
, and
(M_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A}),\langle T^{\infty},\sigma\rangle)
. It is proved that if
(X,T)
is minimal, then
(M_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A}),\langle T^{\infty},\sigma\rangle)
is transitive and has dense minimal points, which can be used to answer some open question.

1.3.
Main results

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s. and let
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
be a family of non-constant integral polynomials. First we will study some basic properties of
((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\sigma)
and
((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\langle T^{\infty},\sigma\rangle)
. It is shown that:

\blacklozenge

\big((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\langle T^{\infty},\sigma\rangle\big)
is ergodic (Theorem
6.1
-(1)).

\blacklozenge

One can characterize the ergodic decomposition of
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
under
\sigma

(Theorem
6.1
-(2), (3)).

\blacklozenge

If the degree of each polynomial in
\mathcal{A}
is greater than 1, then for
\mu
a.e.
x\in X
,
\big((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A},x},\sigma\big)
is a direct product of an infinity-step pro-nilsystem
and a Bernoulli system (Theorem
6.4
).

\blacklozenge

If
(X,T)
is a topological system,
\mu
is an invariant Borel probability measure on
X
with full support,
then
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
is supported on
(N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A}),\langle T^{\infty},\sigma\rangle)
(Theorem
3.7
).

Note that
(N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A}),\langle T^{\infty},\sigma\rangle)
is an equivalent form of
(M_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A}),\langle T^{\infty},\sigma\rangle)
,
see
[
26
, Section 4]
for details.

Also we will use
\big((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\sigma\big)
to study the convergence of multiple averages along polynomials.
Particularly it is proved that

\blacktriangledown

Let
T
and
S
be ergodic measure preserving transformations on a probability space
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
and with
T
having zero entropy. Let
c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
and
p_{i}
be an integral polynomial with
\deg{p_{i}}\geq 2,1\leq i\leq d
. Then for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m},g_{1},\ldots,g_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{1}(T^{c_{1}n}x)\cdots f_{m}(T^{c_{m}n}x)\cdot g_{1}(S^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots g_{d}(S^{p_{d}(n)}x)

exists in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
(Theorem
7.8
).

When
m=d=1
, the result above was given in
[
14
]
. The main point here is that
T
and
S
do not satisfy any commutativity assumptions.

Moreover, we will compare the system defined in this paper with Furstenberg systems of sequences defined in
[
13
,
12
]
(see Section
8
for definitions), and show the following result, which answers Problem 1 in
[
12
]
.

\blacktriangle

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s.,
p
be a non-linear integral polynomial, and
f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
.
Then for any strictly increasing sequence of positive integers
\{N_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
there is a subsequence
\{N^{\prime}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for a.e.
x\in X
the Furstenberg systems of
\big(f(T^{p(n)})x\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
along
\{N^{\prime}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are ergodic and isomorphic to the direct products of infinite-step
pro-nilsystems and Bernoulli systems (Theorem
8.7
).

1.4.
Organization of the paper

We organize the paper as follows. In Section
2
we state some necessary notions and some known facts used in
the paper. In Section
3
, we define
\infty
-joinings
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}

with respect to a given m.p.s. and a family of integral polynomials, and explain them through certain examples. In Section
4
,
we study the
\sigma
-algebra of invariant subsets under the shift map, and show how to relate it to the one in pro-nilfactors. In Section
5
and Section
6
, we give the ergodic decomposition theorem of
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
under the shift map
\sigma
. In the next two sections, we derive some applications of the theory we have developed. Namely, in Section
7
, we provide some new convergence theorems along integral polynomials, and in Section
8
, we compare our notion with Furstenberg systems related to sequences.
In the last section, we present some questions.

Acknowledgments:
We thank Hanfeng Li for useful discussions concerning Theorem
8.6
.
Thank Hui Xu for very careful readings and corrections, and Thank Nikos Frantzikinakis for useful comments.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we give some necessary notions and some known facts used in the paper.
Note that, instead of just considering a single transformation
T
,
sometimes we study commuting
transformations
T_{1}
,
\ldots
,
T_{k}
of
X
. We only recall some basic
definitions and properties of systems for
one transformation, and the extensions to the general case are
straightforward.

2.1.
Some notion in ergodic theory and topological dynamics

2.1.1.
Measurable systems

Recall that in this paper we consider invertible m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
defined on a Lebesgue probability space.
Thus
X
can be given the structure of a compact metric space and
\mathcal{X}
is its Borel
\sigma
-algebra.

For a m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
, the
associated unitary operator (Koopman operator) of
T

is
U_{T}:L^{2}(X,\mu)\rightarrow L^{2}(X,\mu),f\mapsto f\circ T
. In the sequel, we usually denote
U_{T}f
by
Tf
.

For a m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
we write
\mathcal{I}(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
for the
\sigma
-algebra

\{A\in\mathcal{X}:T^{-1}A=A\}
consisting of invariant sets. Sometimes we will use
\mathcal{I}
or
\mathcal{I}(T)
for short.
A m.p.s. is
ergodic
if all
the
T
-invariant sets have measures either
0
or
1
.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
is
weakly mixing
if the product system
(X\times X,\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X},\mu\times\mu,T\times T)
is erdogic.

Let
X,Y
be spaces and
\phi:X\to Y
be a map. For
n\geq 2
let

\phi^{(n)}=\underbrace{\phi\times\cdots\times\phi}_{\text{($n$ times)}}:X^{n}\rightarrow Y^{n}.

For a m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
, we write
(X^{n},\mathcal{X}^{n},\mu^{d},T^{(n)})
for the
n
-fold product system
(X\times\cdots\times X,\mathcal{X}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X},\mu\times\cdots\times\mu,T\times\cdots\times T)
.
The diagonal of

X^{n}
is

\Delta_{n}(X)=\{x^{\otimes n}=(x,\ldots,x)\in X^{n}:x\in X\}.

When
n=2
we write
\Delta(X)=\Delta_{2}(X)
.

A
Bernoulli system
has the form
(Y^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbb{Z}},\nu^{\mathbb{Z}},\sigma)
, where
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu)
is a probability space,
\sigma
is the shift on
Y^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is the product
\sigma
-algebra of
Y^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\nu^{\mathbb{Z}}
is the product measure.

A
factor map
or
homomorphism
from
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
to
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
is a measurable map
\pi:X_{0}\rightarrow Y_{0}
, where
X_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
is

T
-invariant and
Y_{0}\in\mathcal{Y}
is
S
-invariant, both of full measure, such that
\pi_{*}\mu\triangleq\mu\circ\pi^{-1}=\nu
and
S\circ\pi(x)=\pi\circ T(x)
for
x\in X_{0}
. When
we have such a homomorphism we say that
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)

is a
factor
of
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
. If the factor map

\pi:X_{0}\rightarrow Y_{0}
can be chosen to be bijective, then we
say that
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
and
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
are
(measure theoretically) isomorphic
(bijective maps on Lebesgue
spaces have measurable inverses), and denote it by
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)\cong(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
.

Let
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
be a factor of
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
. A factor can be characterized
(modulo isomorphism) by
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{Y})
, which is a
T
-invariant
sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{X}
, and conversely any
T
-invariant
sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{X}
defines a factor. By a classical abuse
of terminology we denote by the same letter the
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{Y}
and its inverse image by
\pi
,
\pi^{-1}(\mathcal{Y})
. In other words, if
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
is a factor of
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
, we think of
\mathcal{Y}
as a
sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{X}
. Thus we may regard
L^{2}(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu)
as a closed subspace of
L^{2}(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
. When there is no danger of confusion, we may abuse the notation and denote the transformation
S
on
Y
by
T
.

We say that
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
is an
inverse limit
of a sequence
of factors
\{(X_{j},\mathcal{X}_{j},\mu_{j},T)\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
if
(\mathcal{X}_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is an increasing
sequence of
T
-invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebras such that

\bigvee_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{X}_{j}=\mathcal{X}
up to sets of measure zero.

2.1.2.
Topological dynamical systems

A
topological dynamical system
(t.d.s. for short) is a pair
(X,T)
,
where
X
is a compact metric space and
T
is a homeomorphism from
X
to itself.
A t.d.s.
(X,T)
is
transitive
if there exists
some point
x\in X
whose orbit
\mathcal{O}(x,T)=\{T^{n}x:n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
is dense
in
X
and we call such a point a
transitive point
. A t.d.s.
(X,T)

is
minimal
if the orbit of any point is dense in
X
. This
property is equivalent to saying that X is the only closed non-empty invariant subset in
X
.

A
topological factor
of a t.d.s.
(X,T)
is another t.d.s.
(Y,S)
such that there exists a continuous and
onto map
\phi:X\rightarrow Y
satisfying
S\circ\phi=\phi\circ T
. In this case,
(X,T)
is called an
topological extension
of
(Y,S)
.
The map
\phi
is called a
topological factor map
.

2.1.3.
{\mathcal{M}}(X)
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{T}(X)

For a t.d.s.
(X,T)
, denote by
{\mathcal{M}}(X)
the set of all Borel
probability measures on
X
. Let
{\mathcal{M}}_{T}(X)=\{\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}(X):T_{*}\mu=\mu\circ T^{-1}=\mu\}
. With the weak
∗
-topology,
{\mathcal{M}}(X)
and
{\mathcal{M}}_{T}(X)
are compact convex spaces. By the Krylov-Bogolioubov
theorem
{\mathcal{M}}_{T}(X)\neq\emptyset
. Denote by
{\mathcal{M}}^{erg}_{T}(X)
the set of ergodic measures of
(X,T)
, then
{\mathcal{M}}^{erg}_{T}(X)
is the set of extreme points of
{\mathcal{M}}_{T}(X)
and one can use the Choquet representation theorem to express each member
of
{\mathcal{M}}_{T}(X)
in terms of the ergodic members of
{\mathcal{M}}_{T}(X)
. That is, for each
\mu\in{\mathcal{M}}_{T}(X)
there is a unique measure

\tau
on the Borel subsets of the compact space
{\mathcal{M}}_{T}(X)
such that
\tau\big({\mathcal{M}}^{erg}_{T}(X)\big)=1
and
\displaystyle\mu=\int_{{\mathcal{M}}^{erg}_{T}(X)}md\tau(m)
,
which is called the
ergodic decomposition
of
\mu
.
In particular,
{\mathcal{M}}_{T}^{erg}(X)\neq\emptyset
.

A t.d.s.
(X,T)
is called
uniquely ergodic
if
there is a unique
T
-invariant Borel probability measure on
X
. It is
called
strictly ergodic
if it is uniquely ergodic and minimal.

2.2.
Topological models

2.2.1.
Topological models

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s. We say that
(\hat{X},\hat{T})

is a
topological model
(or just a
model
) for
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
if
(\hat{X},\hat{T})
is a t.d.s. and there exists an invariant
probability measure
\hat{\mu}
on the Borel
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{B}(\hat{X})
such that the systems
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
and

(\hat{X},\mathcal{B}(\hat{X}),\hat{\mu},\hat{T})
are measure theoretically
isomorphic.

The well-known Jewett-Krieger’s theorem
[
27
,
28
]

states that every ergodic system has a strictly ergodic model.

2.2.2.
Weiss’s Theorem

We say that
\hat{\pi}:\hat{X}\rightarrow\hat{Y}
is a
topological
model
for a factor map
\pi:(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)\rightarrow(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
if

\hat{\pi}
is a topological factor map and there exist measure
theoretical isomorphisms
\phi
and
\psi
such that the diagram

\begin{CD}X@>{\phi}>{}>\hat{X}\\
@V{\pi}V{}V@V{}V{\hat{\pi}}V\\
Y@>{\psi}>{}>\hat{Y}\end{CD}

is commutative, i.e.
\hat{\pi}\phi=\psi\pi
. Weiss
[
42
]

generalized Jewett-Krieger Theorem to the relative case.
Namely, he proved that if
\pi:(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)\rightarrow(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
is a factor map with
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
ergodic and

(\hat{Y},\hat{\mathcal{Y}},\hat{\nu},\hat{S})
is a uniquely ergodic model for
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,T)
, then there is a uniquely ergodic model
(\hat{X},\hat{\mathcal{X}},\hat{\mu},\hat{T})
for
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
and a factor map
\hat{\pi}:\hat{X}\rightarrow\hat{Y}
which is a model for
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
.
We will refer this theorem as
Weiss’s Theorem
.
We note that in
[
42
]
Weiss pointed that the relative case holds
for commutative group actions.

2.3.
The Rohlin skew-product theorem

Let
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
be a m.p.s. and
(U,{\mathcal{U}},\rho)
a probability space. Let
\alpha:Y\rightarrow{\rm Aut}(U,\rho)
be a measurable map (
\alpha
is called a
cocycle
), where
{\rm Aut}(U,\rho)
is the Polish group of all invertible measure-preserving transformations of
(U,{\mathcal{U}},\rho)
. We define the
skew-product system

(Y\times U,\mathcal{Y}\times{\mathcal{U}},\nu\times\rho,T_{\alpha})
as follows:

T_{\alpha}(y,u)=(Sy,\alpha(y)u),\quad\forall(y,u)\in Y\times U.

The following is the well known Rohlin skew-product theorem (see
[
19
, Theorem 3.18]
).

Theorem 2.1

(Rohlin skew-product theorem)
.

Let
\pi:(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)\rightarrow(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
be a factor map of ergodic m.p.s. Then
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
is isomorphic to a skew-product over
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
. That is, there exist a probability space
(U,{\mathcal{U}},\rho)
and a measurable cocycle
\alpha:Y\rightarrow{\rm Aut}(U,\rho)
such that

(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)\cong(Y\times U,\mathcal{Y}\times{\mathcal{U}},\nu\times\rho,T_{\alpha}).

2.4.
Conditional expectations

2.4.1.
Conditional expectations

Let
\pi:(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)\rightarrow(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,T)
be a factor map. As mentioned above,
\mathcal{Y}
is also regarded as a
T
-invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{X}
. For each
f\in L^{1}(X,\mu)
, we write
\mathbb{E}(f|\mathcal{Y})
, or
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}(f|\mathcal{Y})
if needed, for the

conditional expectation
of
f
with respect to
\mathcal{Y}
.
We will frequently make use of the
identities

\int_{X}\mathbb{E}(f|\mathcal{Y})\ d\mu=\int_{X}f\ d\mu\quad\text{and}\quad T\mathbb{E}(f|\mathcal{Y})=\mathbb{E}(Tf|\mathcal{Y}).

We say that a function
f\in L^{2}(X,\mu)

is
orthogonal
to
\mathcal{Y}
, and we write
f\perp\mathcal{Y}
, when it has a zero
conditional expectation on
\mathcal{Y}
.

2.4.2.

The
disintegration of
\mu
over
\nu
, written as
\displaystyle\mu=\int_{Y}\mu_{y}\ d\nu(y)
,
is given by a measurable map

y\mapsto\mu_{y}
from
Y
to the space of Borel probability measures on

X
such that

(2.1)

\mathbb{E}(f|\mathcal{Y})(y)=\int_{X}fd\mu_{y}

\nu
-almost everywhere.

2.5.
Joinings

2.5.1.
Joinings and conditional product measures

The notions of joining and conditional product measure were introduced by Furstenberg in
[
15
]
and
[
16
]
.
Let
(X_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mu_{i},T_{i}),i\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
be m.p.s. A Borel probability measure
\lambda
on

X=\prod_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}
defines a
joining
of the measures on
X_{i}
if
it is invariant under
\prod_{i=1}^{k}T_{i}=T_{1}\times\cdots\times T_{k}
and maps onto

\mu_{j}
under the natural map
\prod_{i=1}^{k}X_{i}\rightarrow X_{j},\forall j\in\{1,2,\ldots,k\}
. When
(X_{1},\mathcal{X}_{1},\mu_{1},T_{1})=(X_{2},\mathcal{X}_{2},\mu_{2},T_{2})=\cdots=(X_{k},\mathcal{X}_{k},\mu_{k},T_{k})
, we then say that

\lambda
is a
k
-fold self-joining
. One can generalize the notion to infinitely many m.p.s. easily.

Let
(X_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mu_{i},T_{i})
be a m.p.s.,
(Y_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i},\nu_{i},S_{i})
be a corresponding factor, and

\pi_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow Y_{i}
be a factor map,
1\leq i\leq k
.
Let
\nu
be a joining of the measures on
Y_{i},i=1,\cdots,k
, and
let
\mu_{i}=\int\mu_{X_{i},y_{i}}\ d\nu_{i}(y_{i})
represent the
disintegration of
\mu_{i}
with respect to
\nu_{i}
. Let
\mu
be a
measure on
X=\prod_{i}X_{i}
defined by

(2.2)

\mu=\int_{Y}\mu_{X_{1},y_{1}}\times\mu_{X_{2},y_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mu_{X_{k},y_{k}}\ d\nu(y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{k}).

Then
\mu
is called the
conditional product measure with
respect to
\nu
.

Equivalently,
\mu
is a conditional product measure relative to
\nu

if and only if for all
k
-tuple
f_{i}\in L^{\infty}(X_{i},\mu_{i}),i=1,\ldots,k

(2.3)

\begin{split}\int_{X}&f_{1}(x_{1})f_{2}(x_{2})\cdots f_{k}(x_{k})\ d\mu(x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{k})\\
&\quad=\int_{Y}\mathbb{E}(f_{1}|\mathcal{Y}_{1})(y_{1})\mathbb{E}(f_{2}|\mathcal{Y}_{2})(y_{2})\cdots\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathcal{Y}_{k})(y_{k})\ d\nu(y_{1},y_{2},\cdots,y_{k}).\end{split}

2.5.2.
Relatively independent joining

Let
(X_{1},\mathcal{X}_{1},\mu_{1},T_{1}),(X_{2},\mathcal{X}_{2},\mu_{2},T_{1})
be m.p.s. and let

(Y,\mathcal{Y},\nu,S)
be a common factor of them with
\pi_{i}:X_{i}\rightarrow Y
for

i=1,2
the factor maps. Let
\mu_{i}=\int_{Y}\mu_{i,y}\ d\nu(y)

represent the disintegration of
\mu_{i}
with respect to
\nu
. Let

\mu_{1}\times_{Y}\mu_{2}
denote the measure defined by

\mu_{1}\times_{Y}\mu_{2}(A)=\int_{Y}\mu_{1,y}\times\mu_{2,y}(A)\ d\nu(y),

for all
A\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}
. The system
(X_{1}\times X_{2},\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2},\mu_{1}\times_{Y}\mu_{2},T_{1}\times T_{2})
is called the

relative product
of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
with respect to
Y
and is
denoted
X_{1}\times_{Y}X_{2}
.
\mu_{1}\times_{Y}\mu_{2}
is also
called
a relatively independent joining
of
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
over

Y
.

2.6.
Furstenberg Joinings

2.6.1.
Furstenberg Joinings

Let
T:X\rightarrow X
be a map and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Set

\tau_{\vec{a}}=T^{a_{1}}\times T^{a_{2}}\times\cdots\times T^{a_{d}},

where
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. For
d\geq 1
let
\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}}
be the measure on
(X^{d},\mathcal{X}^{d})
defined by

\int_{X^{d}}f_{1}(x_{1})\cdots f_{d}(x_{d})d\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=1}^{d}f_{j}(T^{a_{j}n}x)d\mu(x)

for
f_{i}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
,
1\leq j\leq d
, where the limits exists by
[
20
, Theorem 1.1]
.

We call
\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}}
the
Furstenberg self-joining
. Clearly, it is invariant under
\tau_{\vec{a}}
and
T^{(d)}
.

When in addition we assume that
X
is a compact metric space and
T
is a homeomorphism, it is easy to see that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}(\tau_{\vec{a}})_{*}^{n}\mu_{\Delta}^{d}\longrightarrow\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}},\ N\to\infty,\quad\text{weak${}^{*}$ in ${\mathcal{M}}(X^{d})$},

where
\mu_{\Delta}^{d}
is the diagonal measure on
X^{d}
as defined in

[
16
]
, i.e. it is defined on
X^{d}
as follows

\int_{X^{d}}f_{1}(x_{1})\cdots f_{d}(x_{d})\ d\mu_{\Delta}^{d}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=\int_{X}f_{1}(x)\cdots f_{d}(x)\ d\mu(x),

where
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in C(X)
.

2.6.2.
The ergodic decomposition of
\mu_{\vec{a}}^{(d)}

Theorem 2.3
.

[
25
, Theorem D.]

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic system and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}
be distinct non-zero integers. Then there exists a family
\{\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a},x}\}_{x\in X}
of Borel probability measures on
(X^{d},\mathcal{X}^{d})
such that

(1)

the disintegrations of
\mu_{\vec{a}}^{(d)}
over
\mu
is
\displaystyle\mu_{\vec{a}}^{(d)}=\int_{X}\mu_{\vec{a},x}^{(d)}d\mu(x),

(2)

for
\mu
a.e.
x\in X
,
\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a},x}
is ergodic under
\tau_{\vec{a}}=T^{a_{1}}\times T^{a_{2}}\times\cdots\times T^{a_{d}}
,

(3)

for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\prod_{i=1}^{d}f_{i}(T^{a_{i}n}x)\overset{L^{2}(\mu)}{\longrightarrow}\int_{X^{d}}\prod_{i=1}^{d}f_{i}(x_{i})\ d\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a},x}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}),\ N\to\infty.\end{split}

2.7.
Host-Kra seminorms

When
f_{i}
,
i\in I
, are functions on the set
X
, we define a function

\bigotimes_{i\in I}f_{i}
on
X^{I}
by

(2.4)

\bigotimes_{i\in I}f_{i}({\bf x})=\prod_{i\in I}f_{i}(x_{i}),

where
{\bf x}=(x_{i})_{i\in I}\in X^{I}
and
I
is an index set.

2.7.1.
The measure
\mu^{[k]}

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic system and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We define a probability measure
\mu^{[k]}
on
X^{2^{k}}
invariant under
T^{[k]}=T\times T\times\cdots\times T
(
2^{k}
times), by

\mu^{[1]}=\mu\times_{\mathcal{I}(T)}\mu=\mu\times\mu;

and for
k\geq 1
,

\mu^{[k+1]}=\mu^{[k]}\times_{\mathcal{I}(T^{[k]})}\mu^{[k]}.

2.7.2.
Host-Kra seminorms

Write
{\bf x}=(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{2^{k}-1})
for a point of
X^{2^{k}}
, we define a seminorm
\interleave\cdot\interleave_{k}
on
L^{\infty}(\mu)
by

(2.5)

\interleave f\interleave_{k}=\Big(\int_{X^{2^{k}}}\bigotimes_{i\in\{0,1,\ldots,2^{k}-1\}}f({\bf x})d\mu^{[k]}({\bf x})\Big)^{1/2^{k}}=\Big(\int_{X^{2^{k}}}\prod_{i=0}^{2^{k}-1}f(x_{i})d\mu^{[k]}({\bf x})\Big)^{1/2^{k}}.

That
\interleave\cdot\interleave_{k}
is a seminorm
1
1

1

Here for simplicity we give the formula for real functions, and one can give the formula for complex functions similarly.
can be proved as in
[
20
, Lemma 3.2]
, and we call it

Host-Kra seminorm
(HK seminorm for short).
As
X
is assumed to be ergodic, the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{I}^{[0]}
is
trivial and
\mu^{[1]}=\mu\times\mu
. We therefore have

\interleave f\interleave_{1}=\Big(\int_{X^{2}}f(x_{0}){f(x_{1})}d(\mu\times\mu)(x_{0},x_{1})\Big)^{1/2}=\Big|\int fd\mu\Big|.

2.7.3.

It was shown in the proof of
[
20
, Theorem 12.1]
that

Lemma 2.4
.

For every integer
k\geq 0
and every
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, one has

(2.6)

\interleave f\interleave_{k+1}=\Big(\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\interleave f\cdot T^{n}{f}\interleave_{k}^{2^{k}}\Big)^{1/2^{k+1}}.

Note that (
2.6
) can be considered as an alternate definition of the seminorms. For each
f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, one has that
[
20
, Lemma 3.9]

(2.7)

\interleave f\interleave_{k}\leq\interleave f\interleave_{k+1},\ \forall k\in\mathbb{N}.

Lemma 2.5
.

[
25
, Lemma 3.2]

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s. and
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Suppose that
\lambda
is a
d
-fold self-joining of
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
.
Assume that
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
with

\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
for
j=1,\ldots,d
. Then

(2.8)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\Big\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{1}(T^{n}x_{1})f_{2}(T^{2n}x_{2})\cdots f_{d}(T^{dn}x_{d})\Big\|_{L^{2}(X^{d},\lambda)}\leq\min_{1\leq l\leq d}\{l\cdot\interleave f_{l}\interleave_{d}\}

In particular, let
\lambda
be the diagonal joining, and we have

Lemma 2.6
.

[
20
, Theorem 12.1]

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s. and
d\geq 1
be an integer.
Assume that
f_{j}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
with

\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
for
j=1,\ldots,d
. Then

(2.9)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\Big\|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{1}(T^{n}x)f_{2}(T^{2n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{dn}x)\Big\|_{L^{2}(X,\mu)}\leq\min_{1\leq l\leq d}\{l\cdot\interleave f_{l}\interleave_{d}\}

2.8.
Pro-nilsystems

2.8.1.
Nilpotent groups

Let
G
be a group. For
g,h\in G
, we write
[g,h]=ghg^{-1}h^{-1}
for the commutator of
g
and
h
and we write

[A,B]
for the subgroup spanned by
\{[a,b]:a\in A,b\in B\}
.
The commutator subgroups
G_{j}
,
j\geq 1
, are defined inductively by
setting
G_{1}=G
and
G_{j+1}=[G_{j},G]
. Let
k\geq 1
be an
integer. We say that
G
is
k
-step nilpotent
if
G_{k+1}
is
the trivial subgroup.

2.8.2.
Nilsystems

Let
G
be a
k
-step nilpotent Lie group and
\Gamma
a discrete
cocompact subgroup of
G
. The compact manifold
X=G/\Gamma
is
called a
k
-step nilmanifold
. The group
G
acts on
X
by
left translations and we write this action as
(g,x)\mapsto gx
.
The Haar measure
\nu
of
X
is the unique probability measure on

X
invariant under this action. Let
t\in G
and
T
be the
transformation
x\mapsto tx
of
X
. Then
(X,\nu,T)
is
called a
k
-step nilsystem
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. A m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
is called a
k
-step pro-nilsystem
or a
system of order
k
if it is a (measure theoretic) inverse limit of
k
-step nilsystems.

2.8.3.

Here are some basic properties of nilsystems, and they also hold for pro-nilsystems.

Theorem 2.8
.

[
36
,
29
]

Let
(X=G/\Gamma,\nu,T)
be a
k
-step nilsystem with
T
the
translation by the element
t\in G
. Then:

(1)

(X,T)
is uniquely ergodic if and only if
(X,\nu,T)
is
ergodic if and only if
(X,T)
is minimal if and only if
(X,T)

is transitive.

(2)

Let
Y
be the closed orbit of some point
x\in X
. Then
Y
can
be given the structure of a nilmanifold,
Y=H/\Lambda
, where
H

is a closed subgroup of
G
containing
t
, and
\Lambda
is a closed
cocompact subgroup of
H
.

Remark 2.9
.

One can generalize the above results to the action of several translations. For example,
let
X=G/\Gamma
be a nilmanifold with Haar measure
\nu
and let

t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
be commuting elements of
G
. If the group
spanned by the translations
t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}
acts ergodically on

(X,\nu)
, then
X
is uniquely ergodic for this group, see
[
29
]
for details.

2.8.4.
Nilfactors

By the Host-Kra seminorm we can define factors
(Z_{d-1},\mathcal{Z}_{d-1},\mu_{d-1},T)
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s.
For
d\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a
T
-invariant
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{Z}_{d-1}
of
\mathcal{X}
such that for
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
,

\interleave f\interleave_{d}=0\ \text{ if and only if}\ \mathbb{E}(f|\mathcal{Z}_{d-1})=0.

Let
(Z_{d-1},\mathcal{Z}_{d-1},\mu_{d-1},T)
be the factor of
X
associated to the sub
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{Z}_{d-1}
.

By (
2.7
), the factors
Z_{k},k\geq 1,
form an increasing sequence of factors of
X
.
In
[
20
]
, it was showed that
(Z_{d-1},\mathcal{Z}_{d-1},\mu_{d-1},T)
has a very nice structure.

Theorem 2.11
.

[
20
, Theorem 10.1]

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s. and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the
system
(Z_{d-1},\mathcal{Z}_{d-1},\mu_{d-1},T)
is a
(d-1)
-step pro-nilsystem.

2.8.5.
\infty
-step pro-nilsystems

An
\infty
-step pro-nilsystem was introduced and studied in
[
11
]
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s. The factors
\mathcal{Z}_{k},k\in\mathbb{N}
, form an increasing sequence of
T
-invariant sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathcal{X}
, and we let
\mathcal{Z}_{\infty}\triangleq\bigvee_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{Z}_{k}
and
(Z_{\infty},\mathcal{Z}_{\infty},\mu_{\infty},T)
be the factor associated with the
\mathcal{Z}_{\infty}
. Then this system is the inverse limit of the systems
(Z_{k},\mathcal{Z}_{k},\mu_{k},T),k\in\mathbb{N}
. We say
(Z_{\infty},\mathcal{Z}_{\infty},\mu_{\infty},T)
is an
\infty
-step pro-nilsystem
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s. and
f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
.
We define
\interleave f\interleave_{\infty}\triangleq\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\interleave f\interleave_{k}
. Note that

\interleave f\interleave_{\infty}=0
if and only if
\mathbb{E}(f|\mathcal{Z}_{\infty})=0
.

2.9.
Topological pro-nilsystems

2.9.1.

One also has the topological version of this notion, i.e. the topological inverse limit of nilsystems. First recall the
definition of an inverse limit of t.d.s. If

(X_{i},T_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
are t.d.s. with
diam(X_{i})\leq 1
and

\pi_{i}:X_{i+1}\rightarrow X_{i}
are factor maps, the
topological inverse
limit
of the systems is defined to be the compact subset of

\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}X_{i}
given by
\{(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}:\pi_{i}(x_{i+1})=x_{i},i\in\mathbb{N}\}
, and we denote it by

\lim\limits_{\longleftarrow}(X_{i},T_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
. It is a compact
metric space endowed with the distance
\rho((x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}},(y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}})=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}1/2^{i}\rho_{i}(x_{i},y_{i})
, where
\rho_{i}
is the metric in

X_{i}
. We note that the
maps
T_{i}
induce naturally a transformation
T
on the inverse
limit.

Definition 2.13
.

[
24
]

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
. A topological inverse limit of
d
-step minimal
nilsystems is called a
topological
d
-step pro-nilsystem
or a
topological system of order
d
.

See
[
24
]
and
[
38
]
for more about topological pro-nilsystems.

2.9.2.

A minimal t.d.s. is a
topological
\infty
-step pro-nilsystem
if and only if it is
a topological inverse limit of minimal nilsystems
[
11
]
.

By Theorem
2.8
, a topological
d
-step pro-nilsystem is uniquely ergodic for each
d\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Theorem 2.14
.

[
24
, Subsection 5.1]

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Any
d
-step pro-nilsystem is isomorphic in the measure theoretic sense to a topological
d
-step pro-nilsystem.

Lemma 2.15
.

[
11
, Lemma A.3]

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Let
(X,T)
be a topological
d
-step nilsystem,
then the maximal measurable and topological
j
-step pro-nilfactors of
(X,T)

coincide, where
j\leq d
.

2.10.
Nilsequencs

Definition 2.16
.

[
4
]

Let
d\geq 1
be an integer and let
X=G/\Gamma
be a

d
-step nilmanifold. Let
\phi
be a continuous real (or complex)
valued function on
X
and let
t\in G
and
x\in X
. The sequence

\{\phi(t^{n}\cdot x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is called a
basic
d
-step nilsequence
.
A
d
-step nilsequence
is a uniform limit of basic
d
-step
nilsequences.

We will need the following theorem.

Theorem 2.17
.

[
23
, Theorem 16.10]

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. (not necessarily ergodic) and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for all
p\in[1,\infty)
and
\epsilon>0
, every
f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
admits a decomposition

f=f_{\rm unif}+f_{\rm nil}+f_{\rm sml},

where

(1)

the function
f_{\rm unif}
satisfies
\interleave f_{\rm unif}\interleave_{k+1}=0
;

(2)

for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
, the sequence
\big(f_{\rm nil}(T^{n}x)\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a
k
-step nilsequence;

(3)

the function
f_{\rm sml}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
satisfies
\|f_{\rm sml}\|_{L^{p}(X,\mu)}\leq\epsilon
.

Furthermore,
\|f_{\rm nil}\|_{L^{\infty}(X,\mu)}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(X,\mu)}
and
\|f_{\rm nil}+f_{\rm sml}\|_{L^{\infty}(X,\mu)}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(X,\mu)}
.

Remark 2.18
.

(1)

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. As a corollary of Theorem
2.17
,
for all
\epsilon>0
, every
f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
admits a decomposition

f=f_{\rm unif}+f_{\rm nil},

such that for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
, the sequence
\big(f_{\rm nil}(T^{n}x)\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a
k
-step nilsequence and the function
f_{\rm unif}\in L^{\infty}(X,\,u)
satisfies
\interleave f_{\rm unif}\interleave_{k+1}<\epsilon
(
[
23
, Theorem 16.11]
).

(2)

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{k},\mu)
, then for every
\epsilon>0
there is a function
\widetilde{f}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
such that

•

\widetilde{f}\in L^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}_{k},\mu)
,
\|\widetilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mu)}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(X,\mu)}
and
\|f-\widetilde{f}\|_{L^{1}(\mu)}\leq\epsilon
,

•

for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
, the sequence
\big(\widetilde{f}(T^{n}x)\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a
k
-step nilsequence.

(see
[
8
, Proposition 3.1]
)

2.11.
Characteristic factors

In the study of multiple ergodic averages, the idea of characteristic factors plays a very important role. This idea was suggested by Furstenberg in
[
16
]
, and the notion of “characteristic factors” was
first introduced in a paper by Furstenberg and Weiss
[
18
]
.

Definition 2.19
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and
(Y,\mathcal{Y},\mu,T)
be a factor of
X
. Let
\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{d}\}
be a family of integral polynomials,
d\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that
Y
is a
L^{2}
(resp. a.e.)-characteristic factor
of
X
for the scheme
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
if for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
,

\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}&f_{1}(T^{p_{1}(n)}x)f_{2}(T^{p_{2}(n)}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{p_{d}(n)}x)\\
-&\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mathbb{E}(f_{1}|\mathcal{Y})(T^{p_{1}(n)}x)\mathbb{E}(f_{2}|\mathcal{Y})(T^{p_{2}(n)}x)\cdots\mathbb{E}(f_{d}|\mathcal{Y})(T^{p_{d}(n)}x)\to 0\end{split}

in
L^{2}(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
(resp. almost everywhere).

In
[
20
]
the authors showed that the factor
Z_{d-1}
is characteristic for the scheme
\{n,2n,\cdots,dn\}
, and
hence proved that the averages
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{1}(T^{n}x)f_{2}(T^{2n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{dn}x)
converge in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.

2.12.
Furstenberg’s Theorem and Bourgain’s Theorem

2.12.1.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. Let

{\mathcal{H}}_{\rm rat}=\overline{\{f\in L^{2}(X,\mu):\exists a\in\mathbb{N}\ s.t.\ T^{a}f=f\}},

and let
\mathcal{X}_{\rm rat}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
such that

{\mathcal{H}}_{\rm rat}=L^{2}(X,\mathcal{X}_{\rm rat},\mu).

Theorem 2.20

(Furstenberg)
.

[
17
, Lemma 3.14]

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and let
q(n)
be a non-constant integral polynomial. Then for all
f\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
,

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}T^{q(n)}f-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}T^{q(n)}\mathbb{E}(f|\mathcal{X}_{\rm rat})\longrightarrow 0,\ N\to\infty,

in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.
In particular, if
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
is totally ergodic
2
2

2

A m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
is totally ergodic if
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T^{k})
is ergodic for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}T^{q(n)}f\longrightarrow\int_{X}fd\mu,\ N\to\infty,

in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.

Theorem 2.21

(Bourgain)
.

[
6
, Theorem 1]

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and let
q(n)
be a non-constant integral polynomial and
p>1
. Then there exists a constant
C(p,q)>0
such that for all
f\in L^{p}(X,\mu)
,

\Big\|\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}T^{q(n)}f\big|\Big\|_{L^{p}(\mu)}\leq C(p,q)\|f\|_{L^{p}(\mu)}.

Moreover,
\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}T^{q(n)}f
converges almost surely, as
N\to\infty
.

Remark 2.22
.

By Furstenberg’s theorem and Bourgain’s theorem, if

(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
is totally ergodic (in particular, when
T
is weakly mixing), then for all
f\in L^{2}(X,\mu)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}T^{q(n)}f\longrightarrow\int_{X}fd\mu,\ N\to\infty,

converges almost surely.

2.12.2.

By Theorem
2.21
one can show that:

Proposition 2.23
.

[
10
, Corollary 2.2]

Let
T_{1},T_{2},\cdots,T_{d}
be invertible measure preserving transformations acting on a probability space
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
, and let

p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}
be integral polynomials.
Let
q_{1},\ldots,q_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
be positive real numbers such that
\frac{1}{q}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{1}{q_{i}}<1
. Then all the
d
-tuples
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
for which the limit of the averages

(2.10)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{1}(T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}x)f_{2}(T_{2}^{p_{2}(n)}x)\cdots f_{d}(T_{2}^{p_{d}(n)}x)

exits a.e. is closed in
L^{q_{1}}(X,\mu)\times\cdots\times L^{q_{d}}(X,\mu)
.

This proposition says that if
\{(f_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,f_{d}^{(k)})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\prod_{i=1}^{d}L^{q_{i}}(X,\mu)
and the limit of the averages
(\ref {MEA-pol})
exists a.e. for each
(f_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,f_{d}^{(k)}),k\in\mathbb{N}
, then for any limit point
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})
of
\{(f_{1}^{(k)},\ldots,f_{d}^{(k)})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\prod_{i=1}^{d}L^{q_{i}}(X,\mu)
,
\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}f_{1}(T_{1}^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots f_{d}(T_{d}^{p_{d}(n)}x)
exists a.e. Hence by this result, to study
(\ref {MEA-pol})
in
\prod_{i=1}^{d}L^{q_{i}}(X,\mu)
, one may assume that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
or other dense subsets.

2.13.
Multiple polynomial ergodic theorems

The following theorem was proven under some supplementary assumption about the polynomials in
[
21
]
and
was proven in its general form in
[
31
]
. See
[
41
]
for a more general result.

Theorem 2.24

(Multiple Polynomial Ergodic Theorem)
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be an ergodic m.p.s. and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{d}
be integral polynomials. Then for any Følner sequence
\{\Phi_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
in
\mathbb{Z}
and for any
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X)
, the averages

\frac{1}{|\Phi_{N}|}\sum_{{n}\in\Phi_{N}}T^{p_{1}({n})}f_{1}\cdots T^{p_{d}({n})}f_{d}

converge in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
as
N\to\infty
.

The final result we state here is the following

Theorem 2.25
.

[
5
, Theorem A
0
]

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
be a probability space, let
T_{1},\ldots,T_{d}
be commuting measure preserving transformations of
X
, let
p_{1},p_{2},\cdots,p_{d}
be integral polynomials with
p_{1}(0)=\cdots=p_{d}(0)=0
, and let
A\in X
with
\mu(A)>0
. Then

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\mu(T_{1}^{-p_{1}(n)}A\cap T_{2}^{-p_{2}(n)}A\cap\cdots\cap T_{d}^{-p_{d}(n)}A)>0.

3.
Polynomial Furstenberg joinings

In this section we introduce polynomial Furstenberg joinings and give some basic properties.

3.1.
Standard measures on product spaces

In this subsection, we explain why we need to assume that
X
is a compact metric space in a m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
.
For more details see
[
16
]
and
[
17
]
.

3.1.1.

Recall that two probability spaces
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
,
(X^{\prime},\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
are isomorphic if there exist null sets
N\subseteq X,\widetilde{N}\subseteq\widetilde{X}
and an one to one measurable, measure preserving map
\phi:X\setminus N\rightarrow\widetilde{X}\setminus\widetilde{N}
. We say that
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
is a
regular measure space
3
3

3

Here we use definitions from
[
16
,
17
]
. This notion consists with the Lebesgue space used in the previous sections.
if it is isomorphic to a measure space
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}},\widetilde{\mu})
, where
\widetilde{X}
is a compact metric space,
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
the
\sigma
-algebra of Borel sets, and
\widetilde{\mu}
a regular Borel measure (in this case, we say
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}},\widetilde{\mu})
is a
topological model
of
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
). The advantage in dealing with compact metric spaces is that in this situation measures are determined by positive linear functionals on the algebra of continuous functions
C(X)
. If
X
is compact metric, this algebra is separable and the functional is determined by its values on a dense countable set.

A m.p.s.
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
is called
regular
if
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
is a regular measure space. A regular m.p.s, always has a compact metric model
(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}},\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{T})
, where
\widetilde{T}
is a homeomorphism of the compact metric space
\widetilde{X}

[
17
, Theorem 5.1.5]
.

3.1.2.

Let
(X_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mu_{i})
be regular probability spaces,
i\in I
and
I
be a finite or countable index set. Let
\Omega=\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}
with the product
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{B}=\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{X}_{i}
, i.e. the least
\sigma
-algebra with respect to which the projections

\Omega\rightarrow X_{i}
are measurable for all
i\in I
.

A measure
\lambda
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{B})
is called
standard
if its image in each
X_{i}
is
\mu_{i}
.
4
4

4

Let
(X_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mu_{i},T_{i})
be m.p.s., where
i\in I
and
I
is an index set. Then a Borel probability measure
\lambda
on
\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}
is a joining of
\{(X_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mu_{i},T_{i})\}_{i\in I}
if it is a
\prod_{i\in I}T_{i}
-invariant standard measure on
\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}
.
For
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\in I
and
f_{i_{j}}:X_{i_{j}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},1\leq j\leq n
, let
\displaystyle f_{i_{1}}\otimes f_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i_{n}}:\Omega=\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be defined as follows:

(3.1)

f_{i_{1}}\otimes f_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i_{n}}\big((x_{i})_{i\in I}\big)=f_{i_{1}}(x_{i_{1}})f_{i_{2}}(x_{i_{2}})\cdots f_{i_{n}}(x_{i_{n}}).

It is easy to see that if
f_{i_{j}}\in L^{\infty}(X_{i_{j}},\mu_{i_{j}}),1\leq j\leq n
, then
\displaystyle f_{i_{1}}\otimes f_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i_{n}}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega,\lambda)
since
\lambda
is standard.

Note that if each
(X_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mu_{i})
is regular, and
\lambda
on
\Omega
is standard, then
(\Omega,\mathcal{B},\lambda)
is also regular. Let
\{(\widetilde{X}_{i},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{i},\widetilde{\mu}_{i})\}_{i\in I}
be compact metric models of
\{(X_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mu_{i})\}_{i\in I}
. Let
\{O_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{\widetilde{O}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
be null sets in each
X_{i}
and
\widetilde{X}_{i}
respectively such that
\phi_{i}:X_{i}\setminus O_{i}\rightarrow\widetilde{X}_{i}\setminus\widetilde{O}_{i}
is isomorphic. Since
\mu_{i}(O_{i})=0
, we have

\lambda\big(\prod_{i\in I}(X_{i}\setminus O_{i})\big)=1.

Thus
\lambda
may be carried over to a unique regular Borel measure in
\prod_{i\in I}\widetilde{X}_{i}
.

3.1.3.

Now assume that each
X_{i}
is a compact metric space and
\Omega=\prod_{i\in I}X_{i}
. Let

\mathcal{F}=\Big\{\sum_{t=1}^{k}f^{(t)}_{i^{(t)}_{1}}\otimes f^{(t)}_{i^{(t)}_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes f^{(t)}_{i^{(t)}_{n_{t}}}:k\in\mathbb{N},i^{(t)}_{j}\in I,f^{(t)}_{i^{(t)}_{j}}\in C(X_{i^{(t)}_{j}}),1\leq j\leq n_{t},1\leq t\leq k\Big\}.

\mathcal{F}
is a subalgebra of
C(\Omega)
and separates points. By the Stone-Weierstrass theorem,
\mathcal{F}
is dense in
C(\Omega)
. Thus each positive linear functional on
C(\Omega)
is determined uniquely by its value on
\mathcal{F}
. In particular, if we want to define a measure
\lambda
on
\Omega
, we only need to check
\lambda(f_{i_{1}}\otimes f_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{i_{n}})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and for all
f_{i_{1}}\in C(X_{i_{1}}),\ldots,f_{i_{n}}\in C(X_{i_{n}})
.

In the sequel we always assume that
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
is regular, i.e.
X
is a compact metric space.

3.2.
A self-joining
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}

3.2.1.
Some notations

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{d}\}
be a family of non-constant integral polynomials.
We will define a joining
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
on
(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

The point of
(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
is denoted by

{\bf x}=({\bf x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=(\ldots,{\bf x}_{-1},\underset{\bullet}{\bf x_{0}},{\bf x}_{1},\ldots)=\Big((x^{(1)}_{n},x^{(2)}_{n},\ldots,x^{(d)}_{n})\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},

where
``\ \underset{\bullet}{\ }"
means the
0
-th coordinate.
Let
\vec{p}=(p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{d})
and let
T^{\vec{p}(n)}:X^{d}\rightarrow X^{d}
be defined by

(3.2)

T^{\vec{p}(n)}\Big((x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d})\Big)=(T^{p_{1}(n)}x_{1},T^{p_{2}(n)}x_{2},\ldots,T^{p_{d}(n)}x_{d}).

Define
T^{\infty}:(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that

T^{\infty}=\cdots\times T^{(d)}\times T^{(d)}\times\cdots\ (\infty\ \text{times}),
i.e.

T^{\infty}({\bf x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=(T^{(d)}{\bf x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Let
\sigma:(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}

be the shift map, i.e., for all
{\bf x}=({\bf x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
,

(\sigma{\bf x})_{n}={\bf x}_{n+1},\ \forall n\in\mathbb{Z}.

Let
x^{\otimes d}=(x,x,\ldots,x)\in X^{d}
and

\Delta_{\infty}(X)=\{x^{(\infty)}\triangleq(\ldots,x^{\otimes d},x^{\otimes d},\ldots)\in(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}:x\in X\}.

For each
x\in X
, put

(3.3)

\begin{split}\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}&\triangleq(T^{\vec{p}(n)}x^{\otimes d})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\\
&=(\cdots,T^{\vec{p}(-1)}(x^{\otimes d}),\underset{\bullet}{T^{\vec{p}(0)}x^{\otimes d}},T^{\vec{p}(1)}(x^{\otimes d}),T^{\vec{p}(2)}(x^{\otimes d}),\cdots)\in(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}.\end{split}

3.2.2.
An
\infty
-joining with respect to
\mathcal{A}

Now we will define an
\infty
-joining with respect to a given m.p.s.

(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
and
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s.,
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{d}\}
be a set of non-constant integral polynomials. Let
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
be the measure on
(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
defined by

(3.4)

\int_{(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}F({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}F(\sigma^{n}(\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}))d\mu(x)

for all
F\in C((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}})
. We call
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
the
\infty
-joining with respect to
\mathcal{A}
.

Note that the limits exist by the remark below and the equality

\begin{split}\sigma^{n}(\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}})=(T^{\vec{p}(n+j)}x^{\otimes d})_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}=(\ldots,T^{\vec{p}(n-1)}x^{\otimes d},\underset{\bullet}{T^{\vec{p}(n)}x^{\otimes d}},T^{\vec{p}(n+1)}x^{\otimes d},\ldots).\end{split}

Clearly, the measure
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
is invariant under
T^{\infty}
and
\sigma
.

Remark 3.2
.

Now we explain the existence of (
3.4
). Let
f^{\otimes}
be elements of
C(X^{d})
with the following forms:

f^{\otimes}\triangleq f^{(1)}\otimes f^{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f^{(d)},

where
f^{(1)},f^{(2)},\cdots,f^{(d)}\in C(X)
.
By the Stone-Weierstrass theorem, the set

\mathcal{F}=\left\{\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}:l\in\mathbb{N},f^{\otimes}_{1},f^{\otimes}_{2},\ldots,f^{\otimes}_{l}\in C(X^{d})\right\}

is dense in
C((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}})
. Note that by (
3.1
),
\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}
can be regarded as an element of
C((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Thus (
3.4
) holds if and only if for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
f_{j}^{\otimes}=f_{j}^{(1)}\otimes f_{j}^{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(d)}\in C(X^{d}),-l\leq j\leq l
,

(3.5)

\begin{split}&\int_{(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})\\
&=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}(T^{\vec{p}(n+j)}x^{\otimes d})d\mu(x)\\
&=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{(1)}(T^{p_{1}(n+j)}x)f_{j}^{(2)}(T^{p_{2}(n+j)}x)\cdots f_{j}^{(d)}(T^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)d\mu(x),\end{split}

where the limits exist by Theorem
2.24
. Thus
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is well defined.

Another way to see
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
is as follows:

(3.6)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}(\cdots\times T^{\vec{p}(n-1)}\times\underset{\bullet}{T^{\vec{p}(n)}}\times T^{\vec{p}(n+1)}\times\cdots)_{*}\mu_{\Delta}^{\infty}\longrightarrow\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},

as
N\to\infty
in the weak
∗
topology of
{\mathcal{M}}((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}})
, where
\mu_{\Delta}^{\infty}
is the diagonal measure on
(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
as defined as follows

\int_{(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}\Big)({\bf x})\ d\mu_{\Delta}^{\infty}({\bf x})=\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}(x^{\otimes d})\ d\mu(x),

for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
f_{j}^{\otimes}=f_{j}^{(1)}\otimes f_{j}^{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(d)}\in C(X^{d}),-l\leq j\leq l
.

Remark 3.3
.

Note that
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
is a standard measure. To see this fact, let
\Pi:(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow X
be the projection to some coordinate. Let
\widetilde{\mu}=(\Pi)_{*}\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
. Then for all
f\in C(X)
,

\widetilde{\mu}(f)=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}f(T^{q(n)}x)d\mu(x)=\int_{X}fd\mu=\mu(f),

where
q(n)=p(n+m)
for some
p\in\mathcal{A},m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus
\widetilde{\mu}=\mu
, and
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a standard measure.

3.2.3.

Now we show (
3.5
) still holds if we replace the continuous functions by bounded measurable ones, and the definition of
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is independent of the choice of topological models of
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
. That is, we will show

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s., and let
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
be a family of non-constant integral polynomials. Then for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
f_{j}^{\otimes}=f_{j}^{(1)}\otimes f_{j}^{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(d)}
(where
f^{(i)}_{j}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu),1\leq i\leq d,-l\leq j\leq l
)

(3.7)

\begin{split}\int_{(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}(T^{\vec{p}(n+j)}x^{\otimes d})d\mu(x),\end{split}

where
f_{j}^{\otimes}(T^{\vec{p}(n+j)}x^{\otimes d})=f_{j}^{(1)}(T^{p_{1}(n+j)}x)f_{j}^{(2)}(T^{p_{2}(n+j)}x)\cdots f_{j}^{(d)}(T^{p_{d}(n+j)}x).

See Appendix
A
for a proof.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
(X_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mu_{i},T_{i}),i=1,2
, be two isomorphic m.p.s. such that

\phi:(X_{1},\mathcal{X}_{1},\mu_{1},T)\rightarrow(X_{2},\mathcal{X}_{2},\mu_{2},T)

is an isomorphism. Let
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
be a set of non-constant integral polynomials. Then

\phi^{\infty}:((X_{1}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}_{1}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mu_{1})_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\langle T_{1}^{\infty},\sigma\rangle)\rightarrow((X_{2}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mu_{2})_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\langle T_{2}^{\infty},\sigma\rangle)

is also an isomorphism.

Proof.

Note that
\phi^{\infty}
is defined by
\phi^{\infty}({\bf x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=(\phi^{(d)}{\bf x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},\forall({\bf x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in(X_{1}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}.
It is easy to verify that
\phi^{\infty}\circ T_{1}^{\infty}=T_{2}^{\infty}\circ\phi^{\infty}
and
\sigma\circ\phi^{\infty}=\phi^{\infty}\circ\sigma
.

It suffices to show that
\phi^{\infty}_{*}(\mu_{1})_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}=(\mu_{2})_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
. By Proposition
3.4
,
for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
f_{j}^{\otimes}=f_{j}^{(1)}\otimes f_{j}^{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(d)}
(where
f^{(i)}_{j}\in L^{\infty}(X_{2},\mu_{2}),1\leq i\leq d,-l\leq j\leq l
)

\begin{split}&\int_{(X_{2}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}\Big)({\bf x})d(\mu_{2})_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X_{2}}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}(T_{2}^{\vec{p}(n+j)}x^{\otimes d})d\mu_{2}(x)\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X_{2}}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{(1)}(T_{2}^{p_{1}(n+j)}x)\cdots f_{j}^{(d)}(T_{2}^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)d\mu_{2}(x)\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X_{2}}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{(1)}(T_{2}^{p_{1}(n+j)}x)\cdots f_{j}^{(d)}(T_{2}^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)d(\phi_{*}\mu_{1})(x)\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X_{1}}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{(1)}\circ\phi(T_{1}^{p_{1}(n+j)}z)\cdots f_{j}^{(d)}\circ\phi(T_{1}^{p_{d}(n+j)}z)d\mu_{1}(z)\\
&\quad=\int_{(X_{1}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}\Big)\circ\phi^{\infty}({\bf z})d(\mu_{1})_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf z})=\int_{(X_{2}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}\Big)({\bf x})d\big(\phi^{\infty}_{*}(\mu_{1})_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}\big)({\bf x}).\end{split}

The proof is complete.
∎

3.3.
Some special cases

To get a better understanding of
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
, we explain it by some examples.

3.3.1.
The case when
\mathcal{A}=\{p\}
with
p(n)=n

In this case we show that

(3.8)

(X^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\sigma)\cong(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T),

where
\cong
means that two m.p.s. are isomorphic.

Now
p(n)=n
, and we have for
x\in X

\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}=(\ldots,T^{-1}x,\underset{\bullet}{x},Tx,T^{2}x,\ldots)=(T^{n}x)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Note that

\sigma\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}=(\ldots,T^{-1}x,x,\underset{\bullet}{Tx},T^{2}x,\ldots)=T^{\infty}\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}.

For all
l\in\mathbb{N}
, and all
f_{-l},f_{-l+1},\ldots,f_{l}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
,

\begin{split}&\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}f_{-l}(x_{-l})f_{-l+1}(x_{-l+1})\cdots f_{l}(x_{l})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}(\ldots,x_{-1},x_{0},x_{1},\ldots)\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}f_{-l}(T^{n-l}x)f_{-l+1}(T^{n-l+1}x)\cdots f_{l}(T^{n+l}x)d\mu(x)\\
&\quad=\int_{X}f_{-l}(T^{-l}x)f_{-l+1}(T^{-l+1}x)\cdots f_{l}(T^{l}x)d\mu(x).\end{split}

Equivalently, for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
A_{-l},A_{-l+1},\ldots,A_{l}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}(A_{-l}\times A_{-l+1}\times\cdots\times A_{l})=\mu(T^{l}A_{-l}\cap T^{l-1}A_{-l+1}\cap\cdots\cap T^{-l}A_{l}).

That means

(3.9)

\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}=(\cdots\times T^{-1}\times{\rm id}\times T\times T^{2}\times\cdots)_{*}\mu^{\infty}_{\Delta}.

Thus
(X^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)})
is isomorphic to
(X^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\Delta}^{\infty})
, and hence it is isomorphic to
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)
.
That is, if we define

\phi:(X,\mathcal{X},\mu)\rightarrow(X^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}),\ x\mapsto\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}},\ \forall x\in X

then it is an isomorphism between them.
So

(X^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\sigma)\cong(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T).

3.3.2.
The case when
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{d}\}
with
p_{i}(n)=a_{i}n
,
1\leq i\leq d

Let
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{d}\}
with
p_{i}(n)=a_{i}n,1\leq i\leq d
, where
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}
are distinct non-zero integers. In this case we show that

(3.10)

((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\sigma)\cong(X^{d},\mathcal{X}^{d},\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}},\tau_{\vec{a}}),

where
\tau_{\vec{a}}=T^{a_{1}}\times\cdots\times T^{a_{d}}
and
\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}}
is the Furstenberg joining.

As
p_{i}(n)=a_{i}n,1\leq i\leq d
, we have that

\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}=\Big((T^{a_{1}n}x,T^{a_{2}n}x,\ldots,T^{a_{d}n}x)\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}.

Note that

\begin{split}\sigma\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}&=\Big((T^{a_{1}(n+1)}x,T^{a_{2}(n+1)}x,\ldots,T^{a_{d}(n+1)}x)\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\\
&=\Big(\tau_{\vec{a}}(T^{a_{1}n}x,T^{a_{2}n}x,\ldots,T^{a_{d}n}x)\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\tau_{\vec{a}}^{\infty}\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}},\end{split}

where
\tau_{\vec{a}}^{\infty}=\cdots\times\tau_{\vec{a}}\times\tau_{\vec{a}}\times\cdots.

Now we calculate the
\infty
-joining
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
.
For all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
f_{j}^{\otimes}=f_{j}^{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(d)}\in C(X^{d}),-l\leq j\leq l
,

\begin{split}&\int_{(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}(T^{a_{1}(n+j)}x,\ldots,T^{a_{d}(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}(T^{a_{1}}\times T^{a_{2}}\times\cdots\times T^{a_{d}})^{n}\Big(\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}(T^{a_{1}j}x,\ldots,T^{a_{d}j}x)\Big)d\mu(x)\\
&\quad=\int_{X^{d}}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}(T^{a_{1}j}x_{1},\ldots,T^{a_{d}j}x_{d})d\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d})\\
&\quad=\int_{X^{d}}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{(1)}(T^{a_{1}j}x_{1})f_{j}^{(2)}(T^{a_{2}j}x_{2})\cdots f_{j}^{(d)}(T^{a_{d}j}x_{d})d\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}).\end{split}

Equivalently, for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and
A_{j}^{\otimes}=A_{j}^{(1)}\times\cdots\times A_{j}^{(d)}\in\mathcal{X}^{d},-l\leq j\leq l
,

(3.11)

\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}(A_{-l}^{\otimes}\times A_{-l+1}^{\otimes}\times\cdots\times A_{l}^{\otimes})=\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}}\big(\tau_{\vec{a}}^{l}A^{\otimes}_{-l}\cap\tau_{\vec{a}}^{l-1}A_{-l+1}^{\otimes}\cap\cdots\cap\tau_{\vec{a}}^{-l}A^{\otimes}_{l}\big).

Similar to the definition of
\mu_{\Delta}^{\infty}
, we define
(\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}})^{\infty}_{\Delta}
on
(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}
as follows:

\int_{(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}{\bf f}_{-l}({\bf x}_{-l})\cdots{\bf f}_{l}({\bf x}_{l})d(\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}})^{\infty}_{\Delta}(\ldots,{\bf x}_{-1},{\bf x}_{0},{\bf x}_{1},\ldots)=\int_{X^{d}}{\bf f}_{-l}({\bf x})\cdots{\bf f}_{l}({\bf x})d\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}}({\bf x}),

for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
{\bf f}_{-l},\ldots,{\bf f}_{l}\in C(X^{d})
.
It is clear that
((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}})^{\infty}_{\Delta})
is isomorphic to
(X^{d},\mathcal{X}^{d},\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}})
.

Thus (
3.11
) means that

\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}=(\cdots\times\tau_{\vec{a}}^{-1}\times{\rm id}_{X^{d}}\times\tau_{\vec{a}}\times\tau_{\vec{a}}^{2}\times\cdots)_{*}(\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}})_{\Delta}^{\infty}.

It follows that
\big((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}\big)
is isomorphic to
((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}})^{\infty}_{\Delta})
, and hence also isomorphic to
(X^{d},\mathcal{X}^{d},\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}})
.
That is, if we define

\begin{split}\phi:(X^{d},\mathcal{X}^{d},\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}},\tau_{\vec{a}})&\rightarrow\big((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\sigma\big),\\
{\bf y}&\mapsto(\ldots,\tau^{-1}_{\vec{a}}{\bf y},{\bf y},\tau_{\vec{a}}{\bf y},\tau_{\vec{a}}^{2}{\bf y},\ldots),\ \forall{\bf y}\in X^{d}\end{split}

then it is an isomorphism.

3.3.3.
Weakly mixing m.p.s.

(I) Case
\mathcal{A}=\{n^{2}\}

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a weakly mixing m.p.s. and
\mathcal{A}=\{p\}
with
p(n)=n^{2}
. In this case we show that

(3.12)

(X^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\sigma)\cong(X^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{\mathbb{Z}},\sigma),

that is, it is a Bernoulli system.

Recall that for
x\in X
,

\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}=(\ldots,T^{p(-1)}x,\underset{\bullet}{x},T^{p(1)}x,T^{p(2)}x,\ldots)=(T^{p(n)}x)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}.

For all
l\in\mathbb{N}
, and all
f_{-l},\ldots,f_{l}\in C(X)
,

\begin{split}&\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}f_{-l}(x_{-l})f_{-l+1}(x_{-l+1})\cdots f_{l}(x_{l})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}(\ldots,x_{-1},x_{0},x_{1},\ldots)\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\\
&\quad=\big(\int_{X}f_{-l}d\mu\big)\big(\int_{X}f_{-l+1}d\mu\big)\cdots\big(\int_{X}f_{l}d\mu\big),\end{split}

where the last equation holds by
[
2
, Theorem 1.1]
.
Thus
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}=\cdots\times\mu\times\mu\times\cdots=\mu^{\mathbb{Z}}.

(II) Case
\mathcal{A}=\{n,n^{2}\}

Now we study the case when
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},p_{2}\}
with
p_{1}(n)=n,p_{2}(n)=n^{2}
. In this case we show that

(3.13)

((X^{2})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{2})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\sigma)\cong(X\times X^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu\times\mu^{\mathbb{Z}},T\times\sigma).

For
x\in X
,

\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}=\Big((T^{n}x,T^{n^{2}}x)\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}.
Thus, for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
, and all
f_{-l}^{(1)},f_{-l}^{(2)},\ldots,f_{l}^{(1)},f_{l}^{(2)}\in C(X)
,
{\bf x}=\big((x_{n}^{(1)},x_{n}^{(2)})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
,

\begin{split}&\int_{(X^{2})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{(1)}(x_{j}^{(1)})f_{j}^{(2)}(x_{j}^{(2)})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{(1)}(T^{n+j}x)f_{j}^{(2)}(T^{(n+j)^{2}}x)d\mu(x))\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}\big(f_{j}^{(1)}\circ T^{j}\big)(T^{n}x)\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{(2)}(T^{(n+j)^{2}}x)d\mu(x)\\
&\quad=\Big(\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}\big(f_{j}^{(1)}\circ T^{j}\big)d\mu\Big)\prod_{j=-l}^{l}\int_{X}f_{j}^{(2)}d\mu,\end{split}

where the last equation holds by
[
2
, Theorem 1.1]
.
Hence

\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}=\big((\cdots\times T^{-1}\times\underset{\bullet}{{\rm id}}\times T\times\cdots)_{*}\mu^{\infty}_{\Delta}\big)\times\mu^{\mathbb{Z}}.

It follows that
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is isomorphic to
\mu\times\mu^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
If we define

\begin{split}\phi:(X\times X^{\mathbb{Z}},&\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}^{d},\mu\times\mu^{\mathbb{Z}})\rightarrow((X^{2})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{2})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}),\\
&\big(x,(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\big)\mapsto\big(T^{n}x,x_{n}\big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},\ \forall\big(x,(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\big)\in X\times X^{\mathbb{Z}},\end{split}

then it is an isomorphism between them.

3.4.
An equivalent way to see
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}

In previous subsections we see that when
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
with
p_{i}(n)=a_{i}n
, (where
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}
are distinct non-zero integers),
((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\sigma)\cong(X^{d},\mathcal{X}^{d},\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}},\tau_{\vec{a}})
. In this subsection,
we give an equivalent way to see
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
, and it will be more convenient to deal with certain problems when
\mathcal{A}
contains linear elements.

3.4.1.

We assume that
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{s},p_{s+1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
, where
s\geq 0
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{s}
are distinct non-zero integers,
p_{i}(n)=a_{i}n
(
1\leq i\leq s
) and
\deg p_{i}\geq 2,p_{i}(0)=0,s+1\leq i\leq d
.
Recall that

\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}=\Big((T^{a_{1}n}x,\ldots,T^{a_{s}n}x,T^{p_{s+1}(n)}x,\ldots,T^{p_{d}(n)}x)\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}.

For all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
f_{j}^{\otimes}=f_{j}^{(1)}\otimes f_{j}^{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(d)}\in C(X^{d}),-l\leq j\leq l
, denote

f_{j}^{\otimes_{I}}=f_{j}^{(1)}\otimes f_{j}^{(2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(s)},\ f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}=f_{j}^{(s+1)}\otimes f_{j}^{(s+2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(d)}.

Thus
f_{j}^{\otimes}=f_{j}^{\otimes_{I}}\otimes f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}
.
Now

(3.14)

\begin{split}&\quad\int_{(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes}(T^{a_{1}(n+j)}x,T^{a_{2}(n+j)}x,\ldots,T^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes_{I}}(T^{a_{1}(n+j)}x,\ldots,T^{a_{s}(n+j)}x)\cdot\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad f_{l}^{\otimes_{II}}(T^{p_{s+1}(n+j)}x,\ldots,T^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}(T^{a_{1}}\times\cdots\times T^{a_{s}})^{n}\Big(\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes_{I}}(T^{a_{1}j}x,\ldots,T^{a_{s}j}x)\Big)\cdot\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}(T^{p_{s+1}(n+j)}x,\ldots,T^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)d\mu(x).\end{split}

3.4.2.
Definition of
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}

Now we define the new system.

Let
\Omega=X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Define
\widetilde{\sigma}:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
as follow: for

(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{s})\times{\bf x}\in\Omega=X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},

(3.15)

\widetilde{\sigma}\Big((x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{s})\times{\bf x}\Big)=\Big((T^{a_{1}}x_{1},T^{a_{2}}x_{2},\ldots,T^{a_{s}}x_{s})\times\sigma_{II}{\bf x},

where
\sigma_{II}
is the shift on
(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}}
. That is,
\widetilde{\sigma}=\tau_{\vec{a}}\times\sigma_{II}
, where
\tau_{\vec{a}}=T^{a_{1}}\times\cdots\times T^{a_{s}}
.
Let

\xi_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}=\Big((x,\ldots,x),(T^{p_{s+1}(j)}x,\ldots,T^{p_{d}(j)}x)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big)\in X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}}.

Then

\widetilde{\sigma}^{n}\xi^{\mathcal{A}}_{x}=\Big((T^{a_{1}n}x,\ldots,T^{a_{s}n}x),(T^{p_{s+1}(n+j)}x,\ldots,T^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big)\in X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}}.

Note that when
s=0
,
\xi_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}=\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}
.

Next we define a measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
on
\Omega
.
For all
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{s}\in C(X)
, all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}=f_{j}^{(s+1)}\otimes f_{j}^{(s+2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(d)}\in C(X^{d-s}),-l\leq j\leq l
,

(3.16)

\begin{split}&\int_{X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\big(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{s}\big)\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}\Big)((x_{1},\ldots,x_{s}),{\bf x})d\widetilde{\mu}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}(T^{a_{i}n}x)\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}(T^{{p_{s+1}}(n+j)}x,\ldots,T^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}(T^{a_{1}}\times\cdots\times T^{a_{s}})^{n}(f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{s})(x,\ldots,x)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}(T^{{p_{s+1}}(n+j)}x,\ldots,T^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)d\mu(x),\end{split}

where
((x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{s}),{\bf x})=((x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{s}),({\bf x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\in X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

3.4.3.
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
are isomorphic

Compared (
3.14
) with (
3.16
), we see that

((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\sigma)\cong(X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{s}\times(\mathcal{X}^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\sigma}).

That is, if we define

(3.17)

\begin{split}\phi:&(X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{s}\times(\mathcal{X}^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}})\rightarrow((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}),\\
&({\bf y},{\bf x})\mapsto\big(\tau_{\vec{a}}^{j}{\bf y},{\bf x}_{j}\big)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}},\ \forall({\bf y},{\bf x})=\Big({\bf y},\big({\bf x}_{j}\big)_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big)\in X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},\end{split}

then it is an isomorphism.

Note that if
s=0
, then
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
; and if
s=d
, then
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}=\mu^{(d)}_{\vec{a}}
.

3.4.4.
Compare
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
with
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}

We have shown that

((X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\mathcal{X}^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\sigma)
is isomorphic to
(X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}^{s}\times(\mathcal{X}^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\sigma}).

It is clear that the definition of
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is cleaner than
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
, as we do not care whether there are linear terms in
\mathcal{A}
or not. But when we study certain dynamical properties,
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is easier to handle than
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
. For example, in the next section, we use
\widetilde{\mu}^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
in Lemma
4.9
, and it much more complicated if we use
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
instead.

3.4.5.

Similar to Remark
3.3
, we have that
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
is a standard measure.
And similar to the proof of Proposition
3.4
, in (
3.16
) we can replace continuous functions by measurable ones.
That is, we have the following proposition.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and
d\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{s},p_{s+1},\ldots,p_{d}\}
be a family of non-constant integral polynomials, where
s\geq 0
,
p_{i}(n)=a_{i}n
(
1\leq i\leq s
),
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{s}
are distinct non-zero integers, and
\deg p_{i}\geq 2,p_{i}(0)=0,s+1\leq i\leq d
.
For all
f_{1},f_{2},\ldots,f_{s}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, all
l\in\mathbb{N}
and all
f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}=f_{j}^{(s+1)}\otimes f_{j}^{(s+2)}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{j}^{(d)}
(where
f^{(t)}_{j}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
,
s+1\leq t\leq d,-l\leq j\leq l
),

(3.18)

\begin{split}&\quad\int_{X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}}}\big(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}\big)\otimes\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}\Big)((x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{s}),{\bf x})d\widetilde{\mu}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}\\
&\quad=\lim_{N\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{i=1}^{s}f_{i}(T^{a_{i}n}x)\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}^{\otimes_{II}}(T^{{p_{s+1}}(n+j)}x,\ldots,T^{p_{d}(n+j)}x)d\mu(x),\end{split}

where
((x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{s}),{\bf x})=((x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{s}),({\bf x}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}})\in X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Also similar to Proposition
3.5
, if
(X_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i},\mu_{i},T_{i}),i=1,2
, are two isomorphic m.p.s., then

(X_{1}\times(X_{1}^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}_{1}\times(\mathcal{X}_{1}^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\widetilde{\mu_{1}})_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\langle T_{1}^{\infty},\widetilde{\sigma}\rangle)
and

(X_{2}\times(X_{2}^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},\mathcal{X}_{2}\times(\mathcal{X}_{2}^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}},(\widetilde{\mu_{2}})_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)},\langle T_{2}^{\infty},\widetilde{\sigma}\rangle)

are also isomorphic.

3.5.
Topological correspondences

3.5.1.
N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})
and
M_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})

Let
(X,T)
be a t.d.s.
We set

N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})=\overline{\bigcup\{\mathcal{O}(\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}},\sigma):x\in X\}}=\overline{\bigcup\{\sigma^{n}\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}:x\in X,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}}\subseteq(X^{d})^{\mathbb{Z}},

and

M_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})=\overline{\bigcup\{\mathcal{O}(\xi_{x}^{\mathcal{A}},\widetilde{\sigma}):x\in X\}}=\overline{\bigcup\{\widetilde{\sigma}^{n}\xi_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}:x\in X,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}}\subseteq X^{s}\times(X^{d-s})^{\mathbb{Z}}.

It is clear that
N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})
is invariant under the action of
T^{\infty}
and
\sigma
, and

M_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})
is invariant under the action of
T^{\infty}
and
\widetilde{\sigma}
. Thus
(N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A}),\langle T^{\infty},{\sigma}\rangle)
and
(M_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A}),\langle T^{\infty},\widetilde{\sigma}\rangle)
are
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-t.d.s., which were introduced and investigated in
[
26
]
.

3.5.2.

The following theorem indicates the connection between them and the systems we introduced in the current paper.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
({X},T)
be a t.d.s. and let
\mathcal{A}=\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{d}\}
be a family of non-constant integral polynomials with
p_{i}(0)=0,1\leq i\leq d
. Then for all
\mu\in M_{T}(X)
, we have

{\rm supp}\ \mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}\subseteq N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})\ \text{and}\ {\rm supp}\ \widetilde{\mu}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}\subseteq M_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A}).

If in addition
{\rm supp}\ \mu=X
, then
{\rm supp}\ \mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}=N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})
and
{\rm supp}\ \widetilde{\mu}_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}=M_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A}).

Proof.

We only show the first inclusion, and the second one can be proved similarly.

The measure
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
is a weak limit of averages of Dirac masses at points
of the form
\sigma^{n}\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
x\in X
. Since all of these points belong to
N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})
, the measure
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is concentrated on this set, i.e.
{\rm supp}\ \mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}\subseteq N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})
.

It remains to show that
N_{\infty}(X,\mathcal{A})={\rm supp}\ \mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
if
{\rm supp}\ \mu=X
. Since
{\rm supp}\ \mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
is invariant under the actions of
T^{\infty}
and
\sigma
, it suffices to show that for any
x\in X
, we have
\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}\in{\rm supp}\ \mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}
. Thus we need to show that if
U
is an open neighbourhood of
\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}
, then
\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}(U)>0
.
Without loss of generality, we assume that
U=\big(\prod_{j=-\infty}^{-l-1}X^{d}\big)\times\big(\prod_{j=-l}^{l}U^{\otimes}_{j}\big)\times\big(\prod_{j=l+1}^{\infty}X^{d}\big),

where
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
U_{j}^{\otimes}=U_{j}^{(1)}\times U_{j}^{(2)}\times\cdots\times U_{j}^{(d)}
, and
U_{j}^{(t)}
is open in
X
,
1\leq t\leq d,-l\leq j\leq l
. Since
\omega_{x}^{\mathcal{A}}=(T^{\vec{p}(n)}x^{\otimes d})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in U
, we have
T^{p_{t}{(j)}}x\in U^{(t)}_{j}
,
1\leq t\leq d,-l\leq j\leq l
. Choose a non-empty open subset
V
of
X
such that

V\subseteq T^{-p_{t}(j)}U_{j}^{(t)},\ \forall 1\leq t\leq d,-l\leq j\leq l.

Thus we have

\begin{split}\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}(U)&=\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}\Big(\big(\prod_{j=-\infty}^{-l-1}X^{d}\big)\times\big(\prod_{j=-l}^{l}U^{\otimes}_{j}\big)\times\big(\prod_{j=l+1}^{\infty}X^{d}\big)\Big)\\
