\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
. Choose continuous functions
g_{j}
such that

\|g_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
and
\|f_{j}-g_{j}\|_{L^{1}(X,\mu)}<\frac{\epsilon}{2l+1}
for all
-l\leq j\leq l
. Since
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a standard measure, it is easy to see that
\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}\in L^{\infty}(X^{\mathbb{Z}},\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}})
.
We have

(A.8)

\begin{split}&\quad\left|\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\right|\\
&\leq\left|\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})-\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}g_{j}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})\right|\\
&+\left|\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}g_{j}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}g_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\right|\\
&+\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}g_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\right|.\end{split}

Because
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
is a standard measure, by Lemma
A.2
we have

(A.9)

\left|\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}d\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}-\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}g_{j}d\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}\right|\leq\sum_{j=-l}^{l}\int_{X}|f_{j}-g_{j}|d\mu\leq\epsilon.

Since
g_{-l},g_{-l+1},\ldots g_{l}\in C(X)
, by the definition of
\mu^{(\infty)}_{\mathcal{A}}
, we have

(A.10)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\left|\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}g_{j}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}g_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\right|=0.

Since
\mu
is
T
-invariant, for
-l\leq j\leq l
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\begin{split}&\quad\int_{X}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Big|g_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)-f_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)\Big|d\mu(x)\\
&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\Big|g_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)-f_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)\Big|d\mu(x)=\|g_{j}-f_{j}\|_{L^{1}(X,\mu)}.\end{split}

Hence by Lemma
A.1
,

(A.11)

\begin{split}&\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}g_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\right|\\
&\leq\sum_{j=-l}^{l}\Big[\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{X}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Big|g_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)-f_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)\Big|d\mu(x)\Big]\\
&=\sum_{j=-l}^{l}\|f_{j}-g_{j}\|_{L^{1}(X,\mu)}\leq\epsilon.\end{split}

So combining (
A.8
)-(
A.11
), we have

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\left|\int_{X^{\mathbb{Z}}}\Big(\bigotimes_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}\Big)({\bf x})d\mu_{\mathcal{A}}^{(\infty)}({\bf x})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\int_{X}\prod_{j=-l}^{l}f_{j}(T^{p(n+j)}x)d\mu(x)\right|\leq 2\epsilon.

Since
\epsilon
is arbitrary, the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix B
A weighted ergodic average and proof of Theorem
7.6

First we need some notions and results in
[
22
]
. In this section, by an
interval
, we mean an interval in
\mathbb{Z}
. If
I
is an interval,
|I|
denotes its length. Throughout this section,
{\bf I}
denotes a sequence
(I_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
of intervals, and we implicitly assume that the lengths of the intervals tend to
\infty
as
N\to\infty
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
a=(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a bounded sequence of real numbers
and
{\bf I}=(I_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of intervals whose lengths
|I_{N}|
tend to infinity. We say that this sequence of intervals is
k
-adapted to the sequence
a=(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, if for every
\underline{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
,
the limit

(B.1)

c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I},a)\triangleq\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|I_{N}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}a_{n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|I_{N}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}a_{n+\epsilon\cdot\underline{h}}

exists. Note that every sequence of intervals whose lengths tend to infinity admits a subsequence
which is adapted to the sequence
a=(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Definition B.1
.

Suppose that
{\bf I}=(I_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is
k
-adapted to
a=(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. We define

(B.2)

\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I},k}\triangleq\left(\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H^{k}}\sum_{\underline{h}\in[0,H-1]^{k}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I},a)\right)^{1/2^{k}}.

Remark B.2
.

Suppose that
{\bf I}=(I_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is
k
-adapted to
a=(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Using a variant of Furstenberg’s correspondence principle, one can show that there exist a t.d.s.
(X,T)
, a transitive point
w
, a continuous function
f
on
X
, and a
T
-invariant Borel probability measure
\mu
on
X
such that
a_{n}=f(T^{n}w)
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and for
\underline{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
,

(B.3)

c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I},a)=\int_{X}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}f(T^{\epsilon\cdot\underline{h}}x)d\mu(x).

See Subsection 4.2 of
[
22
]
or
[
23
, Proposition 22.1]
for details about this fact. Thus

\begin{split}\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I},k}&=\left(\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H^{k}}\sum_{\underline{h}\in[0,H-1]^{k}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I},a)\right)^{1/2^{k}}\\
&=\left(\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H^{k}}\sum_{\underline{h}\in[0,H-1]^{k}}\int_{X}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}f(T^{\epsilon\cdot\underline{h}}x)d\mu(x)\right)^{1/2^{k}}\\
&=\interleave f\interleave_{k}.\end{split}

It follows that
\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I},k}
is well-defined. We need an equivalent definition of
\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I},k}
. By Appendix A of
[
3
]
or
[
23
, Theorem 8.26]
, we have

\interleave f\interleave_{k}=\left(\lim_{H_{1}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{1}}\sum_{h_{1}=1}^{H_{1}}\cdots\lim_{H_{k}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}\int_{X}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}f(T^{h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}}x)d\mu(x)\right)^{1/2^{k}}.

It follows that

(B.4)

\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I},k}=\left(\lim_{H_{1}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{1}}\sum_{h_{1}=1}^{H_{1}}\cdots\lim_{H_{k}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I},a)\right)^{1/2^{k}}.

Note that by symmetry, the order of
H_{1},\ldots,H_{k}
is not important in the left side of the equation (
B.4
).
We will use this equation later.

Theorem B.3
.

[
22
, Corollary 2.14]

Let
a=(a_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a bounded sequence of real numbers,
and
{\bf I}=(I_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of intervals that is
k
-adapted to this sequence for some
k\geq 2
. Suppose that
\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I},k}=0
.
Then for every bounded
(k-1)
-step nilsequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|I_{N}|}\sum_{n\in I_{n}}a_{n}u_{n}=0.

We will prove a result more general than Theorem
7.6
.

Theorem B.4
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and
k\geq 2
be an integer. Let
(\phi_{n}(x))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be a uniformly bounded sequence of
\mu
-measurable functions such that, for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
, the sequence
(\phi_{n}(x))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a
(k-1)
-step nilsequence. Then for all integral polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}
and for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, the averages

(B.5)

\frac{1}{N-M}\sum_{n=M}^{N-1}\phi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{p_{1}(n)}x)f_{2}(T^{p_{2}(n)}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{p_{d}(n)}x)

converge in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
as
N-M\to\infty
.

Moreover, if the integral polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}
are essentially distinct and
\mathbb{E}(f_{j}|\mathcal{Z}_{\infty}(T))=0
for some
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,d\}
, then the averages (
B.5
) converge to
0
in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
with
\|f_{j}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
for
j=1,\ldots,d
. And we assume that integral polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{d}
are essentially distinct and non-constant with
p_{1}(0)=\cdots=p_{d}(0)=0
.

Step 1.

For all non-zero distinct
c_{1},\ldots,c_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
and for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, if
\interleave f_{j}\interleave_{k+d}=0
for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, then the averages

(B.6)

\frac{1}{N-M}\sum_{n=M}^{N-1}\phi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{c_{1}n}x)f_{2}(T^{c_{2}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{c_{d}n}x)

converge to 0 in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
as
N-M\to\infty
.

In order to prove that (
B.6
) converges to
0
, it suffices to show that every sequence of intervals
{\bf I}=(I_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
whose lengths
|I_{N}|
tend to infinity, admits a subsequence
{\bf I}^{\prime}=(I_{N}^{\prime})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

(B.7)

\frac{1}{|I_{N}^{\prime}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}^{\prime}}\phi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{c_{1}n}x)f_{2}(T^{c_{2}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{c_{d}n}x)\longrightarrow 0,\ N\to\infty,\ \text{in $L^{2}(X,\mu)$}.

For
x\in X
, we write
a(x)=(a_{n}(x))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for the sequence defined by

a_{n}(x)=f_{1}(T^{c_{1}n}x)f_{2}(T^{c_{2}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{c_{d}n}x).

For every
\underline{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we study the averages

\frac{1}{|I_{N}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}a_{n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}}(x).

Note that

(B.8)

\begin{split}a_{n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}}(x)&=\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{1}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{1}\Big)(T^{c_{1}n}x)\ldots\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{d}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{d}\Big)(T^{c_{d}n}x).\end{split}

Thus by Lemma
2.6
7
7

7

In Lemma
2.6
,
I_{N}=[0,N-1]
. It holds for any
{\bf I}=(I_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, a sequence of intervals whose lengths
|I_{N}|
tend to infinity
[
23
, Proposition 21.7]
.

(B.9)

\begin{split}&\limsup_{N\to\infty}\Big\|\frac{1}{|I_{N}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}a_{n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}}(x)\Big\|_{L^{2}(X,\mu)}\\
&\quad\quad\leq C\min_{1\leq j\leq d}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d},\end{split}

where
C
is a positive constant only depending on
c_{1},\ldots,c_{d}
.
By
[
20
, Theorem 1.1]
the averages

\frac{1}{|I_{N}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}a_{n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}}(x)

converge in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
. As a consequence, a subsequence of this sequence of averages converges

\mu
-almost everywhere. This subsequence depends on the parameter
\underline{h}
, but since there are only countably many such parameters, by a diagonal argument we can find a subsequence
{\bf I}^{\prime}=(I_{N}^{\prime})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
the limit

(B.10)

c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},a(x))=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|I_{N}^{\prime}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}a_{n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}}(x)

exists for every choice of
\underline{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. This means that, for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
, the sequence of intervals
{\bf I}^{\prime}
is
k
-adapted to the sequence
a(x)=(a_{n}(x))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
By (
B.9
),

(B.11)

\big\|c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},a(x))\big\|_{L^{2}(X,\mu)}\leq C\min_{1\leq j\leq d}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d}.

Let
\Phi_{H}=[1,H_{1}]\times\cdots\times[1,H_{k}]
,
H_{1},\ldots,H_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that
\interleave f_{j}\interleave_{k+d}=0
for some
j\in\{1,2,\ldots,d\}
. Then by (
B.11
)

(B.12)

\begin{split}&\quad\big\|\frac{1}{|\Phi_{H}|}\sum_{\underline{h}\in\Phi_{H}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},a(x))\big\|_{L^{2}(X,\mu)}\leq\frac{1}{|\Phi_{H}|}\sum_{\underline{h}\in\Phi_{H}}\big\|c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},a(x))\big\|_{L^{2}(X,\mu)}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{|\Phi_{H}|}\sum_{\underline{h}\in\Phi_{H}}C\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d}\\
&=C\cdot\frac{1}{H_{1}}\sum_{h_{1}=1}^{H_{1}}\ldots\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d}.\end{split}

Now we have

\begin{split}&\limsup_{H_{k}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d}\\
\leq&\limsup_{H_{k}\to\infty}\Big(\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d}^{2^{d}}\Big)^{1/2^{d}}\\
=&\limsup_{H_{k}\to\infty}\Big(\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}\interleave(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k-1}\epsilon_{k-1})}f_{j})T^{c_{j}h_{k}}\cdot\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k-1}\epsilon_{k-1})}f_{j})\interleave_{d}^{2^{d}}\Big)^{1/2^{d}}\\
\leq&\limsup_{H_{k}\to\infty}|c_{j}|^{\frac{1}{2^{d}}}\Big(\frac{1}{|c_{j}|H_{k}}\sum_{l=1}^{|c_{j}|H_{k}}\interleave(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k-1}\epsilon_{k-1})}f_{j})T^{l}\cdot\\
&\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994pt(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k-1}\epsilon_{k-1})}f_{j})\interleave_{d}^{2^{d}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2^{d}}}\\
=&|c_{j}|^{1/2^{d}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k-1}\epsilon_{k-1})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d+1}^{2},\end{split}

where the inequality holds by using the inequality
(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}B_{n}^{k_{1}})^{1/k_{1}}\leq(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}B_{n}^{k_{2}})^{1/k_{2}}
, for all
B_{1},\ldots,B_{N}>0
and
k_{1}\leq k_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
, the last equality is by Lemma
2.4
.

Similarly, we have

\begin{split}&\limsup_{H_{k-1}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k-1}}\sum_{h_{k-1}=1}^{H_{k-1}}\limsup_{H_{k}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d}\\
&\quad\leq|c_{j}|^{1/2^{d}}\limsup_{H_{k-1}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k-1}}\sum_{h_{k-1}=1}^{H_{k-1}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k-1}\epsilon_{k-1})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d+1}^{2}\\
&\quad\leq|c_{j}|^{1/2^{d}}\limsup_{H_{k-1}\to\infty}\left(\Big(\frac{1}{H_{k-1}}\sum_{h_{k-1}=1}^{H_{k-1}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k-1}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k-1}\epsilon_{k-1})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d+1}^{2^{d+1}}\Big)^{1/2^{d+1}}\right)^{2}\\
&\quad\leq|c_{j}|^{1/2^{d}}\Big(|c_{j}|^{1/2^{d+1}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k-2}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k-2}\epsilon_{k-2})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d+2}^{2}\Big)^{2}\\
&\quad=|c_{j}|^{1/2^{d-1}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k-2}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k-2}\epsilon_{k-2})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d+2}^{2^{2}}.\end{split}

Repeating this progress several times, we have

\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{1}}\sum_{h_{1}=1}^{H_{1}}\cdots\limsup_{H_{k}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{a_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d}\leq|c_{j}|^{\frac{1}{2^{d-k+1}}}\interleave f_{j}\interleave_{d+k}^{2^{k}}.

Combining with (
B.12
), we have

(B.13)

\begin{split}&\quad\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}\cdots\limsup_{H_{k}\to\infty}\big\|\frac{1}{|\Phi_{H}|}\sum_{\underline{h}\in\Phi_{H}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},a(x))\big\|_{L^{2}(X,\mu)}\\
&\leq\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{1}}\sum_{h_{1}=1}^{H_{1}}\cdots\limsup_{H_{k}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}C\interleave\Big(\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}T^{c_{j}(h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k})}f_{j}\Big)\interleave_{d}\\
&\leq C|c_{j}|^{\frac{1}{2^{d-k+1}}}\cdot\interleave f_{j}\interleave_{d+k}^{2^{k}}.\end{split}

By Remark
B.2
,

\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I}^{\prime},k}=\left(\lim_{H_{k}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}\cdots\lim_{H_{1}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{1}}\sum_{h_{1}=1}^{H_{1}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},a)\right)^{1/2^{k}}.

Thus by Hölder inequality and by (
B.13
), we get

\begin{split}&\quad\left\|\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I}^{\prime},k}\right\|^{2^{k}}_{L^{2}(X,\mu)}=\left(\int_{X}\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I}^{\prime},k}^{2}d\mu\right)^{2^{k-1}}\leq\int_{X}\interleave a\interleave_{{\bf I}^{\prime},k}^{2^{k}}d\mu\\
&=\int_{X}\left(\lim_{H_{k}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}\cdots\lim_{H_{1}\to\infty}\frac{1}{H_{1}}\sum_{h_{1}=1}^{H_{1}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},a)\right)d\mu\\
&=\lim_{H_{1}\to\infty}\ldots\lim_{H_{k}\to\infty}\int_{X}\left(\frac{1}{H_{1}}\sum_{h_{1}=1}^{H_{1}}\cdots\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h_{k}=1}^{H_{k}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},a)\right)d\mu\\
&\leq\limsup_{H_{1}\to\infty}\cdots\limsup_{H_{k}\to\infty}\big\|\frac{1}{|\Phi_{H}|}\sum_{\underline{h}\in\Phi_{H}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},a(x))\big\|_{L^{2}(X,\mu)}\\
&\overset{\eqref{app8}}{\leq}C|c_{j}|^{\frac{1}{2^{d-k+1}}}\cdot\interleave f_{j}\interleave_{d+k}^{2^{k}}.\end{split}

Hence we have

(B.14)

\Big\|\interleave a(x)\interleave_{{\bf I}^{\prime},k}\Big\|_{L^{2}(X,\mu)}\leq C^{\frac{1}{2^{k}}}|c_{j}|^{\frac{1}{2^{d+1}}}\cdot\interleave f_{j}\interleave_{d+k}=0.

Thus for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
,
\interleave a(x)\interleave_{{\bf I}^{\prime},k}=0
. By Theorem
B.3
,
we have

\frac{1}{|I_{N}^{\prime}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}^{\prime}}\phi_{n}(x)a_{n}(x)\to 0,N\to\infty,\ \mu\text{-almost everywhere},

and (
B.7
) is proved. This completes the proof of Step 1.

Step 2.

For all all non-zero distinct
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
and for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, the averages

\frac{1}{N-M}\sum_{n=M}^{N-1}\phi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{a_{1}n}x)f_{2}(T^{a_{2}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{a_{d}n}x)

converge in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
as
N-M\to\infty
.

By Step 1, for all non-zero distinct
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
and for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, if
\interleave f_{j}\interleave_{k+d}=0
for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
, then the averages

\frac{1}{N-M}\sum_{n=M}^{N-1}\phi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{a_{1}n}x)f_{2}(T^{a_{2}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{a_{d}n}x)

converge to 0 in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.
Thus we can restrict to the case where
f_{j},1\leq j\leq d
, is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{Z}_{m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

By Remark
2.18
-(2), for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\widetilde{f}_{j}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
such that
\widetilde{f}_{j}
is
\mathcal{Z}_{m}
-measurable and
\|f_{j}-\widetilde{f}_{j}\|_{L^{1}(X,\mu)}<\epsilon,1\leq j\leq d
, and for
\mu
-almost all
x\in X
the sequence

(\widetilde{f}_{j}(T^{n}x))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an
m
-step nilsequence.
Thus
(\Psi_{n}(x))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{d}\widetilde{f}_{j}(T^{a_{j}n}x)\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a nilsequence.
Therefore, it suffices to show the existence in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
of the limit

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\phi_{n}(x)\Psi_{n}(x).

This follows from Theorem
2.8
since both are nilsequences. The proof of Step 2 is complete.

Step 3.

For all non-zero distinct
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
and essentially distinct non-linear integral polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{s}
for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d},g_{1},\ldots,g_{s}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, there is some
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
\interleave g_{j}\interleave_{m}=0
for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
, then the averages

(B.15)

\frac{1}{N-M}\sum_{n=M}^{N-1}\phi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{a_{1}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{a_{d}n}x)\cdot g_{1}(T^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots g_{s}(T^{p_{s}(n)}x)\rightarrow 0

in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
as
N-M\to\infty
.

In order to prove (
B.15
), it suffices to show that every sequence of intervals
{\bf I}=(I_{N})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
whose lengths
|I_{N}|
tend to infinity, admits a subsequence
{\bf I}^{\prime}=(I_{N}^{\prime})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

(B.16)

\frac{1}{|I_{N}^{\prime}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}^{\prime}}\phi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{a_{1}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{a_{d}n}x)\cdot g_{1}(T^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots g_{s}(T^{p_{s}(n)}x)\rightarrow 0,\ N\to\infty

in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.

For
x\in X
, we write
b(x)=(b_{n}(x))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
for the sequence defined by

b_{n}(x)=f_{1}(T^{a_{1}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{a_{d}n}x)\cdot g_{1}(T^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots g_{s}(T^{p_{s}(n)}x).

For every
\underline{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
, we study the averages

(B.17)

\frac{1}{|I_{N}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}b_{n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}}(x).

Let
D=\{\underline{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}:

p_{j}(n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}),1\leq j\leq s,\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}
are essentially distinct integral polynomials
\}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{k}
. By
[
26
, Lemma 6.1]
, if
p_{1},p_{2}
are two essentially distinct integral polynomials of degree
\geq 2
, then there is a positive constant
L
depending on
p_{1},p_{2}
such that whenever
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
with
|k_{1}-k_{2}|\geq L
, the integral polynomials
p_{i_{1}}(n+k_{j_{1}})-p_{i_{1}}(k_{j_{1}})
and
p_{i_{2}}(n+k_{j_{2}})-p_{i_{2}}(k_{j_{2}})
in one variable
n
are essentially distinct for each
(i_{1},j_{1})\neq(i_{2},j_{2})\in\{1,2\}\times\{1,2\}
. By this fact, it is easy to see that
D
has zero density in
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.

For all
\underline{h}\in D
, since
p_{j}(n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}),1\leq j\leq s,\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}
are essentially distinct integral polynomials with degree
\geq 2
, by Theorem
4.1
there is some integer
m
(depending only on the weight of polynomials
\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{s}\}
) such that
(
B.17
) converges to
0
in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.

Similar to the proof of Step 1, there exists a subsequence
{\bf I}^{\prime}=(I_{N}^{\prime})_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
the limit

(B.18)

c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},b(x))=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{|I_{N}^{\prime}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}}\prod_{\epsilon\in\{0,1\}^{k}}b_{n+h_{1}\epsilon_{1}+\cdots+h_{k}\epsilon_{k}}(x)=0

for every choice of
\underline{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in D
. This means that, for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X
, the sequence of intervals
{\bf I}^{\prime}
is
k
-adapted to the sequence
b(x)=(b_{n}(x))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},b(x))=0
for every
\underline{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in D
.
Therefore for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X

\interleave b(x)\interleave_{{\bf I}^{\prime},k}=\left(\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H^{k}}\sum_{\underline{h}\in[0,H-1]^{k}}c_{\underline{h}}({\bf I}^{\prime},b(x))\right)^{1/2^{k}}=0,

since
D
has zero density in
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
.
By Theorem
B.3
,
we have

\frac{1}{|I_{N}^{\prime}|}\sum_{n\in I_{N}^{\prime}}\phi_{n}(x)b_{n}(x)\to 0,N\to\infty,\ \mu\text{-almost everywhere},

and (
B.16
) is proved. This completes the proof of Step 3.

Step 4.

For all non-zero distinct
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
and essentially distinct non-linear integral polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{s}
for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d},g_{1},\ldots,g_{s}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, the averages

(B.19)

\frac{1}{N-M}\sum_{n=M}^{N-1}\phi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{a_{1}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{a_{d}n}x)\cdot g_{1}(T^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots g_{s}(T^{p_{s}(n)}x)

converge in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
as
N-M\to\infty
.

By Step 3, for all non-zero distinct
a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
and essentially distinct non-linear integral polynomials
p_{1},\ldots,p_{s}
for all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d},g_{1},\ldots,g_{s}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, there is some
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that if
\interleave g_{j}\interleave_{m}=0
for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,s\}
, then the averages

(B.20)

\frac{1}{N-M}\sum_{n=M}^{N-1}\phi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{a_{1}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{a_{d}n}x)\cdot g_{1}(T^{p_{1}(n)}x)\cdots g_{s}(T^{p_{s}(n)}x)

converge to 0 in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
.
Thus we can restrict to the case where
g_{j},1\leq j\leq s
, is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{Z}_{m}
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

By Remark
2.18
-(2), for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\widetilde{g}_{j}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
such that
\widetilde{g}_{j}
is
\mathcal{Z}_{m}
-measurable and
\|g_{j}-\widetilde{g}_{j}\|_{L^{1}(X,\mu)}<\epsilon,1\leq j\leq s
, and for
\mu
-almost all
x\in X
the sequence

(\widetilde{g}_{j}(T^{n}x))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an
m
-step nilsequence.
By
[
31
, Proposition 3.14]
or
[
23
, Theorem 14.15]
, for
\mu
-almost all
x\in X
the sequence
(\widetilde{g}_{j}(S^{p_{j}(n)}x))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a
(m\deg{p_{j}})
-step nilsequence for
1\leq j\leq s
. Let
X_{1}\in\mathcal{X}
be a full measure subset of
X
for which this property holds.
Thus
(\Psi_{n}(x))_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}=\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{s}\widetilde{g}_{j}(T^{a_{j}n}x)\Big)_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a nilsequence.
Therefore, it suffices to show the existence in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
of the limit

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\phi_{n}(x)\Psi_{n}(x)f_{1}(T^{a_{1}n}x)\cdots f_{d}(T^{a_{d}n}x)

This follows from Step 2 since
\phi_{n}(x)\Psi_{n}(x)
is a nilsequence. The whole proof is complete.
∎

Appendix C
A lemma about topological models

The proof of the following result was outlined in
[
16
]
. So for completeness, we give a proof here, following the proof of
[
17
, Theorem 5.15]
and
[
19
, Theorem 2.15]
.
Theorem
C.1
is a little stronger than Theorem
8.6
.

Theorem C.1
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)
be a m.p.s. and let
\{f_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
. Then there is a t.d.s.
(X^{\prime},T^{\prime})
, a
T^{\prime}
-invariant measure
\mu^{\prime}
and an isomorphism
\psi:(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)\rightarrow(X^{\prime},\mathcal{B}(X^{\prime}),\mu^{\prime},T^{\prime})

such that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
f^{\prime}_{k}=f_{k}\circ\psi^{-1}\pmod{\mu^{\prime}}
, where
f_{k}^{\prime}\in C(X^{\prime})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
f_{k}
is real-valued function for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, since
f_{k}\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu)
, there is an interval
[a_{k},b_{k}]\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and a measurable function
g_{k}:X\rightarrow[a_{k},b_{k}]
such that
f_{k}(x)=g_{k}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
. Let
\{I^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a countable base of
[a_{k},b_{k}]
. Let
B^{(k)}_{i}=g_{k}^{-1}(I^{(k)}_{i}),i\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\{B^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a countable subset of
\mathcal{X}
.

Now choose a sequence
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of distinct elements of
\mathcal{X}
such that:

(1)

The sequence
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is dense in the measure algebra
(\mathcal{X},\mu)
, and separate points of
X
(i.e. for every
x\neq x^{\prime}
there is an
A_{n}
with
x\in A_{n}
but
x^{\prime}\not\in A_{n}
);

(2)

\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is invariant under
T
, i.e. there is a permutation
\theta:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that
T^{-1}A_{n}=A_{\theta(n)}
;

(3)

\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\{B^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the countable algebra generated by
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Now put
X^{\prime}=\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
,
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}(X^{\prime})
and
A_{n}^{\prime}=\{\xi\in X^{\prime}:\xi(n)=\xi_{n}=1\}
. For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mu_{N}
be the probability measure defined on the finite space
\{0,1\}^{N}
by

\mu_{N}(A^{\prime}_{n_{1}}\cap A^{\prime}_{n_{2}}\cap\cdots\cap A^{\prime}_{n_{s}})=\mu(A_{n_{1}}\cap A_{n-2}\cap\cdots\cap A_{n_{s}}),1\leq n_{1}<n_{2}<\ldots<n_{s}\leq N.

Let
\mu^{\prime}_{N}
be any extension of
\mu_{N}
to a Borel probability measure on
X^{\prime}
. Let
\mu_{N_{j}}^{\prime}\to\mu^{\prime},j\to\infty
be a weak
∗
convergence subsequence. The measure
\mu^{\prime}
satisfies the equalities above for all finite sequences
n_{1}<n_{2}<\ldots<n_{s}
, i.e.

\mu^{\prime}(A^{\prime}_{n_{1}}\cap A^{\prime}_{n_{2}}\cap\cdots\cap A^{\prime}_{n_{s}})=\mu(A_{n_{1}}\cap A_{n-2}\cap\cdots\cap A_{n_{s}}),\ \forall 1\leq n_{1}<n_{2}<\ldots<n_{s}.

The map
\Psi(A_{n}^{\prime})=A_{n},\forall n\in\mathbb{N}
can be extended to an isomorphism of measure algebra
\Psi:(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\rightarrow(\mathcal{A},\mu)
, where
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is the sub-algebra of
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}=\mathcal{B}(X^{\prime})
determined by
\{A_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. The permutation
\theta
defines a homeomorphism
T^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime},\xi\mapsto T^{\prime}\xi
,
where

(T^{\prime}\xi)_{n}=\xi_{\theta(n)},\ \forall n\in\mathbb{N}.

Since
T^{-1}A_{n}=A_{\theta(n)}
, by the definition of
T^{\prime}
it follows that
(T^{\prime})^{-1}A^{\prime}_{n}=A^{\prime}_{\theta(n)}
. Thus we have that
T^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X^{\prime}
is measure-preserving, and
(X^{\prime},\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is a m.p.s.

Next define a map

\psi:X\rightarrow X^{\prime},\ \big(\psi(x)\big)_{n}=1_{A_{n}}(x).

Since the sequence
\{A_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
separates points of
X
,
\psi
is a Borel 1-1 map of
X
into
X^{\prime}
with
\Psi=\psi^{-1}
. Let
X_{0}^{\prime}=\psi(X)
. By Lusin-Souslin Theorem
[
33
, Corollary 15.2]
,
X_{0}^{\prime}

is a Borel subset of
X^{\prime}
and
\psi
is a Borel isomorphism of
X
with
X_{0}^{\prime}
. Note that
\mu(X)=\mu^{\prime}(X_{0}^{\prime})=1
and
(\psi\circ T)(x)=(T^{\prime}\circ\psi)(x),\forall x\in X
. Thus
\psi:(X,\mathcal{X},\mu,T)\rightarrow(X^{\prime},\mathcal{X}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},T^{\prime})
is an isomorphism.

Now for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
f^{\prime}_{k}=g_{k}\circ\psi^{-1}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow[a_{k},b_{k}]
, we have that for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

(f_{k}^{\prime})^{-1}(I^{(k)}_{i})=(g_{k}\circ\psi^{-1})^{-1}(I^{(k)}_{i})=\psi(g_{k}^{-1}(I^{(k)}_{i}))=\psi(B^{(k)}_{i})\in\{A_{n}^{\prime}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

is an open subset of
X^{\prime}
. Since
\{I^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be a countable base of
[a_{k},b_{k}]
,
f_{k}^{\prime}
is continuous. Note that each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
f_{k}(x)=g_{k}(x)
for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in X
, and it follows that
f^{\prime}_{k}(x^{\prime})=f_{k}\circ\psi^{-1}(x^{\prime})
for
\mu^{\prime}
-a.e.
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
.
The proof is complete.
∎
